LADIES' LETTER.

••••••IMEIN••••

UT DUB Nimus, Speaking of sleeves reminds me that a charming one brought to my,notice yesterday was in the bishop style, but quite moderate in size, and on the top of the arm below the elbow there is an opening in V form, which shows a stimulated undersleeve of striped or tucked material. This underpart is arranged on the lining, and a pretty effect can be obtained in a linen or cotton bodice by having the undersleeve and vest of striped cotton, and the overaleeve of plain material. For a blouse in white or pale-coloured silk the tight understeeve can be striped with velvet in black or colour, and the opening over the cuff trimmed round with lace. A black crepe or other soft material makes up very prettily in this style, with an undersleeve of cream lace mounted Olit a white lining.

Cool blouses can J made soft white muslin. The inevitable tuckings are useful in giving them strength and also in keeping them in shape. One I saw 'has an embroidered collar, the second an embroidered yoke, and the sleeves of both widen at ere wrist. The first hat is trimmed with roses, and the second is of "pancake" shape, with a small wreath of ivy and small white heather for trimming. Small flowers and leaves have sne- ceeded,,though not entirely ousted, the enormous ones that have been fashionable for so long A dainty bat is pale tan straw, trimmed with a wreath of bluebells. Another has a garland of ripe corn round the crown and an immense bow of poppy•pink ease. The simplicity of such com- positions as these is all in their favour. No one should ever look as if much intentness of care had been lavished on the toilette. The idea conveyed is not a dignified one.

A nice way of making up cheap muslins is with a skirt edged with several gathered frills and a full bodice-draped with a frill-bordered fichu of the material, arranged to show a small pointed chernisette of either lace or tucked mousseline. Or the bodice may be quite devoid of trimming, different collars and fichus of embroidered net, muslin, and lane being in this case worn over it, according to thelancy of the moment; indeed, if the wearer ism-girt with a good figure, a stock-tie of glace silk vrilt be sufficient finish for ordinary occasions. White sprigged muslin is again to the fore, and very pretty and inexpensive it is. It is really better, -however, .to lauythe muslin by the yard, and have& made. up:at home in a simple, picturesque styie. The ,skitt ran be frilled or trimmed withivradrof lace, and the bodice may have either a rousbn 'bolero edged with lace and worn over a gauze or eitiffon blause-vest or be made in Sam style with lace chemisette. These white frocks look nice with elbow-sleeves, and sashes of pale-coloured chine or plain soft satin ribbon.

prett.

A very useful and ,y blouse is made in black siik, with ivory vest and under-sleeves, and the fancy collar of the ivory silk is appliqué with cream lace motifs. This blouse is arranued on 'a fitted lining of thin texture, which fastens in front, but the right side of the blouse is cut with a sloped extension, which fastens over to the left side, and there is a velvet bow at bust and waist. It is now essential that the costume for a journey, although it need not be expensive, shoald be fairly new, neat, and, above all, suitable. It should, for instance, have a skirt of reasonable length that clears the ground well all round, and which has a sensibly-placed pocket; the jacket should be made so that it will really button up closely if required, and the blouse shoull be free from lace or chiffon trimmings, which look mere raga after a railway journey of a few hours. This summer liaen is a favourite material for travelling dresses, and, although it creases rather too easily to be quite the ideal fabric for the purpose, it certainly looks very nice, and is cool yet sub- stantial. In our ciielate, however, a long journey should not be undertaken in a linen costume with- out a woollen wrap of some kind to put on over it - should the weather turn chilly towards evening. Neglect of this precaution has caused many a bad cold ere now. A dark blue linen skirt, plainly stitched round the hem, and a short double- breastoid reefer coat with either a round or a sailor makes an exceedingly neat travelling costume, especially if it is a little smartened with a detach- able over-collar of embroidered eoru lawn or can vas and heavy linen guipure. The best blouse to wear with this costume would be a soft silk one, which might be eitherikolueer ecru or a pattern in prettily blended colours. White cambric or silk shirts have always a nice appearance with linen coats, bin they get woefully soiled in the train. Your loving Jemrie.

SUNSET AND LIGHTING-UPTIME.—A poist of some interest to cyclists and others was decided at the Scar- borough Mice Court on Monday. James Spence. an ex-police official, wss summoned for driving a vehicle after sunset without being provided with a lighted lamp. According to • constable, the offence was cons_ mitted at 9.37 p.m. on the Bth inst. The defendant die puted the accuracy of the statement to the extent of four minutes. and entered the further plea that there was a vast difference between the times of Greenwich and Edinbumh, and that the sun had not actually set at Scarborough at the time masted. The magistrates dis- missed the summons.

Her eyes slowly came back from that world where she had been, and she saw me at last. I could see the recognition cotnimr like wnter that move' under ice; but no light of love came with it, only a sudden, bleak gleam of horror—or hate, I dared not think which.

"You—you!" she said. "If it had not been for you—" Her voice died, then rose again, chokingly. "To atone—" she cried, aloud. 'Sheila—save—find—you must find--' Her breath went with a gasp. She pressed her hand over her heart, half sitting up, her face con- vulsed with pain. I slipped an arm behind her houlders and laid her gently down again. She yielded • but as her head touched the pillow once more she raised both hands, laying one os each of my shoulders; and then I saw a terrible thing that almost forced a scream from my lips. I held it back by force, but my forehead was wet, and a wave of sickness swept over me.

My mother invariably wore long sleeves with her evening dresses, though sometimes, as to-night, they were of thickly shirred chiffon or gauze.

I knew the reason for this whim of -hers—for a whim those in ignorance of the truth might have called it. On one of those beautiful white arms of hers there was a thing to hide—the same thing which that other woman had so ostentatiously displayed in the theatre. Now, as she lifted her arms, the sleeve fell bat 2 from the right one, cut and torn from wrist shoulder, spotted here and there with crimson. And where the purple scar had been there was a fresh, raw burn, as if the flesh had been cauterised with red-hot iron.

"The West Wing," she panted. "The West Wing." I knew that she strove to say something that she could not utter, and I tried to help. "The West Wing, at home ? " I asked. "Is there something I can do for you there ? " She answered only with her eyes. They seemed to say: " Y es—a hundred times yes " And having said it, they closed. Her hands slid from my shoulders, and, faint and shuddering, I softly laid the poor hurt arm down by her side. As I did so, Swift came forward. I had not heard her enter the room.

"Go and tell them to send for a doctor," I exclaimed. "Quick—she is very ill." Swift went away again. It seemed to me that she was gone a long time, though I do not suppose it could have been more than five or six minutes. I bathed my mother's forehead and hands with the eau de Cologne, but she did not speak or open her eyes. "Have you seen her ill in this way before?" I asked Swift, when she had returntd. "Never so bad," the woman answered, in an awed tone. "But she has suffered with attacks of palpitation of the heart sometimes. She told me was never to mention it to you, and I haven't till nosy. There's some medicine in the next room, I've dropped for her more than once. I think it's called digitalis. Shall I get it now, and try what it will do?"

I nodded assent; and presently Swift came back , with a small bottle.

"The label's been torn off lately," the frightened maid announced. "I can't remember what the dose was. I daren't give it to her ladyship, miss. And, oh, only look at her! Her teeth are locked together. It would be hard to get her to swallow anything now." Somehow the time passed, and the doctor came. Afterwards, I heard that scarcely twenty minutes had gone by between the moment when Swift left me to take the message downstairs and that when he arrived. But the minutes seemed ten times twenty. The doctor was an elderly man, with grey hair and keen eyes, and a quick, concise way of speaking that inspired one with confidence. He asked questions as to my mother's previoui state of health, smelt the medicine in the unlabelled bottle, and put it aside. Evidently it was not what was needed now.

He sounded the heart with his stethoscope, abruptly ordering Swift to get brandy and bring it to him as quickly as she could. Bending his face down, with his ear at the instrument, I saw his eyes suddenly fall upon the fresh burn on my mother's arm. For her sake I could have wished to hide it from him, for I knew how sensitive she had been regarding the scar, what anger she had shewn when once, as a child, I had tactlessly asked her to tell me what it was, and how it came to be there. But I had resisted the impulse to cover up the spot and conceal it from the doctor, thinking that the injury —which must have caused great pain and shock— might partially account for my mother's condition. "What's this ?" the old man brusquely asked, turning with a slight frown to me. "How did she burn herself ?"

"I —don't know," I stammered. "She has been out, away from me for several hours, and only came in a few minutes before we sent for you." "Humph I" he muttered, his bushy brows drawing close together. "She has had a shock, and—the heart is very weak. You are her daughter?" I nodded; for tears choked my voice. "I'm afraid I ought to warn you, then, that she is in great danger. If the action of the heart---" Swift's return interrupted him. But I knew what he would have said, as well as if he had finished the sentence.

"Cut the other sleeve open," he directed the maid. In an instant it was done, and with a tiny hypodermic syringe he injected brandy into the left arm.

This he did again and again, each time raising a slight lump with a tiny dot of red on the top. while I watched in silence, my heart beating fast in my throat. At last her lips moved. "The—West Wing! " she said again, with a fluttering breath. The words ended in a long sigh. A curious, indescribable 'change came over her features. Never had she been so beautiful. Never had she looked, since I had known her, so thoroughly at peace. The doctor's hand was on her breast, his eyes on the wonderful, marble face. For a long moment he did not move. Suddenly, my mother's long lashes quivered, and her eyes opened wide, black as mountain tarns at midnight. Whatever she had seen before, when she had seemed to look through and beyond me, she now saw, or thought she saw, once more. "Mother!" I cried out, sharply. "What is it— oh 1 what is it ? " "My child," said the old doctor, "your mother has gone to sleep. She will not wake again in this world." She was dead. And she had died seeing It !

A GIRL OF THE PEOPLE.

By MRS. C. N. WILLIAMSON,

Author of "The Barn Stormers," "Fortune's Sport," "Miss Nobody," "Her Royal High. nese," "Lady Mary of the Dark House," Ste.

SUMMARY OF OPENING CHAPTERS Sheila Cope, in her first season, is taken by her mother to see Sir Henry Irving in "The Bells." During the first interval her attention is attracted to two persons. One is a young man in the pit, neither well-dressed nor remarkably handsome, but Sheila is indefinably drawn to him. The other is a woman in the stalls, who stares up at the box with a curious expression in her eyes. Having fixed Sheila's attention she removes her gloves and exhibits a curious purple scar on her arm. Sheila is startled, for a similar scar on the arm of her mother has been a mystery to her from childhood. Her mother also notices the woman with the scar, and becomes strangely agitated. She rushes from the theatre, and mother and daughter are just in time to see the mysterious woman get into a f our- wheeler and drive away. Lady Cope puts Sheila into a cab, and, in spite of the girl's protestations, gives orders to drive to the hotel, she herself remaining behind. Acting on a sudden desperate impulse, Sheila directs the cabman to follow the four-wheeler of the woman with the scar, instead of proceeding to the hotel. The cab goes slowly on till a vehicle passes it in an opposite direction. A ray of light striking the window shews Sheila a thing so strange that she is near crying out in her astonishment. Lady Cope and the woman with the scar are in the vehicle. Sheila's cab follows, but loses track of the others; and the girl discovers that her driver is intoxicated. Frightened, she leaves the vehicle, and tries to find her way home on foot. A lurking thief, who has seen her pay the cabman, attacks her with a view to robbery, but in the nick of time a hansom drives up, the thief flies, and from the cab descends the young man of the Lyceum pit. He confesses that having seen Sheila and her mother leaving the theatre, he followed, thinking they must be in trouble of some sort. Before he leaves Sheila, having put her into his own cab, he says that the whole of his reason for follow- ing her and her mother can never be told. Sheila drives home in great perplexity, a l very anxious about her mother.

===== 0002601_19010719_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER 111. MY MOTHER COMES MOMS.

By this time my mother might have arrived at the hotel, to find me playing truant. If this were the case, I could picture her anger, and I shrank from the thought, for the beautiful, sad woman whom I loved knew how to make herself feared as well. Yet I prayed that she might have been before me, and was ready to endure her reproaches rather than hear that she was still absent.

I fancied suppressed amazement on the face of the hall-porter who helped me from the hansom, and handed up my fare to the cabman. "Do you know if Lady Cope has come in yet?" I ttaked, nervously. "I've only just come on duty, at midnight, miss," the hall-porter replied. "I haven't seen her ladyship." I felt unpleasantly sure that the big man suspected that I had been guilty of some imprudent escapade, which I was anxious that "her ladyship" should not discover, and from the good-natured twinkle in his eyes, as he held the door open and watched me fly towards the lift, he probably wished me luck. _ .

But in an instant I had forgotten him and his conjectures. I asked no more questions as to my mother's return, but hurried straight to our rooms on the second floor. _ _

I threw my cloak over the back of a•chair, and knocked at my mother's door, softly calling her name in a voice that would tremble.

There was no answer, and I turned the handle to peep in. Her room was in darkness. Perhaps, I thought, she had returned, and gone straight to bed, taking it for granted that I was safely in the house, and not wishing, for reasons of her own, to see me until morning.. I ventured to switch on the light, which was near the door. The bed was empty, the cover temptingly turned down, a silk night-dress and robe de chambre laid ready by the maid, who would be waiting at this very moment for her delayed

...... There was just one hope left, and I pressed the bell connected with the little room occupied by my mother's maid, Swift. In a moment she appeared. "Oh, miss," she began, "I was just beginninc, to be afraid something was the matter. Her ladyship maid you would be coming straight back after the theatre."

"Then she hasn't sent any word ? " I asked. This had been the one hope I had still relied upon. "No, miss. No word has come for me. Isn't her ladyship with you ? " "She—she has gone with—a friend," 1 faltered, trying to make my voice sound natural, for mother was not one who would tolerate having her private affairs discussed by a servant. "I came back alone. But—l thought she would have been at home by this time, or let me know that she was not to be expected till later. No doubt it is all right. Only I—l can't help feeling a little anxious." "Oh, if her ladyship is with a friend, there's nothing to worry about," Swift cheerily assured me. She had been with us for a long time, ever since I was quite a child, in fact, and I would have given much to ask her a few discreet questions, for it was just possible that she was more in mother's confidence than I was. But I put the thought behind me, and steeled myself in patience. "You look as white as a ghost, miss," the maid went on. "I do hope you haven't got a chill. Better have a cup of chocolate, and let me get you to bed. By the titne you're ready, her ladyship's sure to be here." "I'm not at all cold, and I'm not sleepy," I protested. "I don't feel like being undressed yet, thank you. you can go to bed, and when Lady ' Cope comes I will do everything that she needs. If she should be ill, or really want you, why, of course, I will ring, and you can slip into a dressing-gown. " Swift murmured gratefully that she was "much obliged for my kindness," but though she decorously retired, there was that in her eye which told me she did not mean to avail herself of my permission. I had spoken calmly, I flattered myself, but I had not been able to conceal from her experience that something unusual had occurred, and doubtless she intended to wait, clothed and alert, for any emergency. Never shall I forget the next hour. Though I had said I was not cold, I shivered with a nervous trembling, and mechanically sat down by the fire, stretching out my hands towards the blaze, which Swift had stirred into renewed life. On the mantel stood a small clock, that ticked in a whisper, and every quarter-hour uttered a silver note. The hands scarcely seemed to move; days have passed more swiftly for me than those lagging quarter'hours. I imagined my mother trapped by a villainous plot into which she had fallen ; murdered even, it might be—for crude tragedy did break into peaceful lives, even in this opening of an enlightened century. As I so thought, the deep silence of the room was ruffled by a faint, all .but inaudible, sound. It was as if someone outside had brushed against the handle of the door in going by. I sprang up, and. cunning across the room, threw the door open, just in time to catch my mother in my arms as she swayed and fell forward. She was a Juno in height and build, while I was a slender girl, whose forehead mune on a level with her chin, if we stood side by side; yet— somehow I held her. I laid her down, and sank on my knees beside her. I was quivering all over, and for a moment was powerless to move. When I could rise, I rang the bell for Swift, and rushed to my own room for eau de Cologne, I was back again before the maid could come, bating my mother's forehead, and begging her to speak, in a strange, weak little voice, that did not sound like my own. Suddenly she opened her eyes —those wonderful eyes, with the haunting shadow that hid their depths. She was gazing straight into mine, yet I knew that she did not see me, and felt, with an eerie chill, that she was looking through Me at something else. So unearthly was the expression of the white face that involuntarily I turned my head over my shoulder, half expecting to see that someone had stolen noiselessly into the room, to stand close behind me. But no one was there. Her lips were moving stiffly. I bent over her as she lay among the cushions, and her breath, as it rustled past my cheek, was cold, or I fancied it. "The scar—the scar!" she was whispering. "After—all—these years. What a fearful revenge! It is—my death." 4411Snther!" I cried, my voice breaking into s sob. "Don't say that; you shall not die. I am kete—vour Sheila."

CHAPTER:IV.

A SOUND AT TIIII DOOR. Only one thing I recall distinctly in connection with that night, after the moment that brought the knowledge and shock of my mother's death. It 'Seemed a trifle to stand out, while other matters, niore important, perhaps, receded into the barb ground of my mind; yet the great events of life are reared on a foundation of trifles.

When my mother's beautiful dead body, in all its bravery of mist-grey crepe and glittering stars of steel, was lifted from the sofa where I had laid her down to die, I noticed something that had passed unobserved before, in the excitement of her coming and her sudden illness. I had half carried, half dragged her from the door to the lounge, after she had fallen against me, Tainting, without removing the cloak that lay loosely over her shoulders, and when I placed her on the sofa, the wrap lay between her and the nest of cushions.

When they took her away it was still there, and as I stood staring dazedly at the place where she had been it was stulldenly borne in upon me that this cloak was not the one which she had worn to the theatre; I had never seen it before.

She had 'gone out in a long, primrose-tinted wrap of satin, brocaded with great marigolds in shining threads of gold. She came home to die in a short wrap of lustreless black silk, lined with a deep shade of purple. I shuddered at sight of the thing, remembering the black, jetted dress worn by the woman with the scar on her arm, the woman whom I could not help secretly regarding as my mother's murderess. I could not bear to see it lying where mother had lain, and in a sudden passion I seized the black, purple-lined garment and flung it viciously frr— the sofa into a far corner of the sitting-room, where it lay in a heap on the floor. Next day my cousin, Roger Cope, came. He had inherited my father's title long ago, and was Sir Roger Cope. An estate which went with the baronetcy had also gone to him; but he had never lived there. The old home by the sea, in Doreetshire, which I loved, had been my mother's. She and my father were ch.tant cousins, and , though Roger had inherited the title, as next-of- kin, after my father's death, he was even more nearly related to my mother. Very little money had gone to Roger with the title and estates, for my father had been poor, my mother rich. and the new Sir Roger Co m let his

place in Dorsetshire, living in town, lie was a aolicitor, and since mother and 1 had come to London for my first season, we had been twice invited to tea at his delightful old chambers in the 2enaple. When we were ready to leave the Coburg Hotel— Swift and I—for the sad journey down to Dorset- shire, the maid came to me at the last moment,

carrying the black, purple-lined cloak in her hand. "What is, it miss ? " she asked. "I never saw

it before. It can't be yours—or my poor lady's. Was it—what she wore home that night—by mistake, perhaps? " "Yes," I answered. night--" I was about to add that the thing should be left in the hotel, where perhaps the owner would one day come to inquire for it, when I remembered that I might reproach myself later for letting a possible clue to the woman in black pass out of my hands. "Give the wrap to me," I said instead. "I should like to look at it." Swift laid the sombre folds over my arm, and - examined the purple lining for a pocket. There was one on each side, that on the left long and narrow, for the reception of a fan; that on the right only large enough for a purse and a handkerchief.

" She wore it that

I plunged my hand into one after the other, and at first thought that both were empty. But into the corner of the purse-pocket was pushed a tiny, crumpled bit of paper. I pulled it out, smoothed it between my fingers, and saw that I had possessed myself of a small slip cut from the column of a newspaper. "Lady Cope and her only daughter, Miss Sheila Cope," I read, "are spending the season at the Coburg Hotel, in Carlos-place." This gave me a new idea. I rang and sent for the manager. Of him I asked if it would be possible to find out, even now, whether anyone had called to see my mother while we were at the theatre on the evening of her death. In a few moments word came back that a lady

had called. She had seemed greatly disappointea at hearing that Lady Cope was out, and had inquired where she might be found. Our box. for the theatre had been ordered by telephone from the hotel, therefore the lady had been informed that we were at the Lyceum. The lady had been tall, dark, dressed and cloaked in black, and she had come in a cab. She had left no name, and nothing further was known about her. But it was enough to serve as an answes to the question I had asked. The woman in black had gone to the theatre in the expectation of seeing my mother, and for that purpose alone. The meeting had not come about by chance. No one save the doctor who had attended my mother, Swift, and myself, knew that anything mysterious had happened that last night. Though the doctor had asked a question or two, and frowned in a puzzled way at the burn on her arm, he had not hesitated to certify that she had died from an acute attack of the heart disease which had troubled her, menacing her life for years. Any sudden shock would have aggravated this disorder, and the doctor was certain that some such shock she must have received. Beyond this he knew notitina, save that she had been absent from home for a few hours. Swift was aware only that her mistress had not come back with me, and had returned later in a dying condition, while my knowledge was not of a kind to establish proof of foul play against any person or persons unknown. I did not even believe that there had been actual foul play. I thought that there was a mystery; that some news of a disturbing nature had been revealed to my mother ; that she had learnt some secret which had come upon her with a great shock. I wished to find the woman in black, not through a hope of bringing her to justice, but because I was sure that my mother had tried to repeat to me the secret she had herself heard. I could think of nc way of learning it, except from the woman who had shown the heart-shaped scar; and though I shrank with physical loathing from the thought of seeing her again, with her cat-eyes and wicked smile, still my reason told me that I must endeavour to do so if I could.

itly mother had liked Roger Cope, but I did not like him; and I could not bring myself to confide in him, establishing a new intimacy between us by relating to him the events of that unhappy night. I resolved to depend upon myself alone, and when I found the newspaper cutting in the pocket of the cloak, discovering, too that the probable owner had called at the hotel, I would not encourage the impulse to rid myself of the hateful garment. I determined to take it away with me to the country, and decide later upon a way of using it as a clue to the mystery. Perhaps I ought to have applied to Scotland Yard, calling in the assistance of the police. But I was reluctant to do this, for I felt that such a course would be abhorrent to my mother if she could know. Besides, there was really little to tell which could make the case seem an important one. So I kept the cloak, already vaguely forming a plan in my mind concerning it. We went drearily down into the country, taking my mother's body; and Roger Cope went with us and was very kind. Then came the funeral; and it was on the night which followed that my memory begins again ty paint vivid pictures of events. I was tired out. A pall of desolation had fallen upon my spirits and I had gone early to my room. Old friends of my mother's had come to me and tried to be kind, but it was a great relief to be alone. _ _ _

I had thought that when I had sent Swift away and was quite by myself, above all things in the world it would do me good to cry—to cry till I should be spent with crying. But when I stood by the window, in my long white cashmere dressing-gown, with only the cold glass between me and the furious storm, the relief I had been looking forward to would not come. I had no longer any desire for tears. I could not even concentrate my mind upon my sorrow. Suddenly I thought of the West Wing and the broken words my mother had stammered. Had she meant that I was to search for something and find it in the West Wing ? The West Wing was the oldest portion of a very old house; but there was nothing mysterious or secret about it, so far as I knew,

The chapel was there, unused for many a year. There was a great picture gallery, which was also ballroom, and had a gallery for musicians at one end. There were three or four little Avainscotted rooms which had once been sacred to the family priest as long ago as the days of Queen Mary. These connected with the chapel by a private stair- case, and my mother had used the prettiest root in the suite, octagon-shaped and mullion-windowed, for a study. She had liked writing letters and reading there; and when she had been in the "octagon room" it was tacitly understood that she ,was "not at home." Nobody was allowed to break in upon her writing or other studies.

Tliere were other rooms also in the West Wing; huge, lofty bedchambers, only occupied when we had a house-party, a thing which seldom happened ; !though once in a while, when Roger came for the shooting, people had been asked, to please him. Old servants, who had lived at Arrish Mel! Court in my father's time, soon after his marriage with my mother, could tell of gay doings, and the West Wing rooms full of guests, who Armed in the picture- gallery below nearly every night. But my father had died abroad when I was a baby, and we were travelling on the Continent, I had been told; so that the West Wing had few bright associations for me.

If my mother had meant to send me on a mission to the West Wing to discover something of importance to her interest or my own, I thought ' that, without doubt, I should begin my quest in the octagon-room. Probably many papers of hers were kept there, and only to-day I had come into possession of a bunch of keys which unlocked, among other things, the drawers of the old escritoire at which she often wrote. I would have given almost anything if she haa been able to finish the sentence broken short death, for I could not bear to feel that there was an ardent wish of hers which I might not be able to carry out. But I could only do my best to guess at her meaning, groping in the dark; and as the sound of her voice rang in my ears like an echo from the past, I was pricked to eagerness in the desire to carry out her instructions—at once, with- out delay. It was not yet eleven o'clock and no time would be better for my quest than night, when there would be no prying eyes about, no whispering tongues. To-morrow, at half-past ten, Roger was coming to Arrish Mell Court, from the inn at the village of Lull, close by, where he was spending the night. He had said that he wanted to have a talk with me on business of importance; and, as he had been my mother's lawyer for the past few years, I fancied it might have to do with money matters. I had begged for a respite of a few days before business of sow sort need be discussed. but Roger had urzed

that everything must remain at a summon= well the matter had been thrashed out betvreen adding that he was only stopping at Lull for the, purpose; so that I had consented to see him, if. would probibly keep me talking long, and would have to be invited to luncheon (since life must IA on in the usual way, even in a house of sadness); therefore, if I put off my visit to the West Wing now 1 muSt wait, at all events, until to-morreif afternoon. I had sent Swift to bed, and I was sure that 1/111 other servants had gone to their rooms also— probably fast asleep by this time, for it had be • long, tiring day, and we were very early people $t Arrish lklell Court.

I took up a branching silver stick, holding tire candles, from my mantel, and went into the corridor outside my bedroom, which was dinitf lighted for the night by one hanging lamp. At the end of this corridor was a baize door, which opened into the West Wing. I was not a cowardly or superstitious girl, for my mother had brought me up in stern creeds, and I had no fear of going at night into a part of the house which happened to be unoccupied. Witb. out a quiver of the nerves I opened the baize door, and moved from a region of comparative light into one which would have been dark but for my two' flaring candles. I went on, threading my wall' through several intricate passages, past the empty, closed bedchambers, until finally I arrived at tht door of the rooms which had been sacred to priest and chaplain. There was a legend that these and the private staircase which led down to the disused chapel were haunted by the uneasy ghost of a certain priest, dead in the days of Oliver Cromwell. He wag supposed to have betrayed the master of the house to the enemy, and killed himself in remorse, ever after walking by night up and down the stairs or through his old rooms, telling his beads amid wringing his hands. But I had almost forgottes this story, and there was no thought of it in mf mind as I grasped the handle of the door. Suddenly, as I was about to open it, I hese,* faint stirring on the other side.

WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

The Question of Finance.

===== 0002601_19010719_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
A Way out of the Difficulty.

From time to time we have published interviews With a local manufacturer who has placed before our readers his views on local topics under the Pseudonym of " Stisy Bee." Coming to the town as a stranger he has pointed out what appeared to him the weak points of our municipal administra- tion and has euegested remedies. Some of his sug- gestions have been or are being carried into effect. Knowing the strong position he has taken as to the necessity of a new bridge over the Mersey, a repor- ter of The Examiner" waited upon him this week to get his opinion on the rejection of Mr Webstet'e scheme for the canstructien of a single- span steel bridge in substitution of the existing bridge, "I have read very carefully," he said " all the arguments which have been adduced in oopositien I to the scheme for a new bridge, but I must confess I that they are not very convincing. I beartily con- cur in the remarks of the Mayor on this important subject. If the Council were of opinion that a new bridge was neceeiary when there was a prospect of Obtaining a portion of the cost from the County Council, it is hardly square or businesslike for them now to turn round and to refuse to face the diffi- culty because the financial burden will fail upon the Corporation. I hope the decision at the last meeting will not be considered final. The subject le one of pressing importance, and is of far treater urgency than even the erection of a new Police station, technicil schools or a small-pox hospital. A new bridge is wanted if the tramway scheme is to be a success. The present bridge is like the neck of a bottle through which the traffic of five converging streets pours. I fail to See the reason of any further widening of Bridge-street .tureitll steps have been taken to construct a new bridg l cantilevers on the existing gee, wohue gldßou fg :Cot c it ya n. iy be a temporary ex- pedient for delaying the construction of a new bridge, for in the course of a few years the Corpora- tion would be called upon to do what I contend they ought to d) new, and that is to or ct a single span bridge at that point. A single span bridge will have an advantage which the present one has not. Warrington is only just beginning to realise the valuable asset it has in the river for carrying put- Poses, and I believe the upper reaches of the river beyond the present bridge might be more largely Used for this purpose if it were not for the obstruction to traffic by Warrington bridge. The two but; resses erected in the bed of the river great- ly impede navigation. The Corporation themselves are sufferers in this respect for they have a wharf at Howley which could be more convenisntly used if there wete erected a single span bridge. Then, again, what is the value of the agreement with the Manche-ter Ship Canal Company as to the mainten- ance of a 41redged channel if no effort is made to remove the existing obstacles in the river." But what about the financial considerations? You caanot have a new bridge constructed under £20,000 " Well, the whole question resolves itself in*o a financial one. Everyone surely must see the neces- sity of a wider bridge, but the recent rise of Sd in the .£ on the rates has rather frightened the Council. lam not at all satisfied with the present way in which the finances of the town are conductel." In what direction would you suggest reform ? "The present system of municipal finance, although it may benefit legal gentlemen,is not right towards the great mass of the ratepayers who are entitled to receive the best efforts of the Chairman and members of the Finance Committee and the Borough Treasurer. It has been admitted, I think, that quite recently there was a temporary overdraft on the tank of something like £120,000 upon which - interest at, the rate of about 41 per cent, was being Charged. I temporary overdraft of such magnitude and at such a high rate of interest is not good finance. In fact it is very bad business, and Would not be tolerated for a week in many towns." What is being done elsewhere' in this matter? " I am coming to that. In all progressive towns where the liabilities are large owing to the numer- ous municipal undertakings it has been found ad- vantageous to create municipal stock I could name 50 first-class municipalities where this method has ben adopted. With your assistance I iive advocated it in Warrington for several years now but, no move has yet been made in that direc- tion. The C oporation are still advertising for loans. I was told a few years ago that the Corpora- tion were able to negotiate loans at 2t per cent in- terest. That was not so bad but I am certain that the , Corporation are not borrowing money at anything , like that rate now. The average rate of municipal stocks is 3 per cent and I feel sure that a consider- able saying cook' be effected in Warrington by the creation of stock. If other mueicipalittes have found it advantageousewhy should not Warrington? 'They have had the same difficulties to face and un- less there was something to be gained they would not have trcubled themselves about Corpora- tion stock. The result of the present system is the payment of a high rate of bank interest whilst the process of negotiation iq going on and the payment afterwards of a higher rate of interest on loans than Would be necessary in the case of municipal stock. Such a scheme as I am advocating cannot be carried out in a day. It requires to be very carefully thought out and put on the market at the right moment." And do you think the savirg effected would be anything like the amount required to pay interest and sinking fund on a new bridge? " The coat of the new bridge to the rat-payere Neve estimated to be a penny in the .£ on the retes. That amount, I believe, could be saved by the crea- tion of municipal stock. There is an excellent opportunity for the Finance Committee to do a go..d stroke of business for the ratepayers. I believe a longer period for the repayment of a large pot tion of the money borrowed could be obtained Tie Municipal balance sheet just issued by the Bcrolgh Treasurer shows a consid,rable excess of assets over liabilities, and proves that the Co, poration are not, anly able to meet, all liabilities, but that the pros •nt generw ion is being called Up^rt Z,o pay more than a fair share for the improveuatn;,B which will be handed dowo to posterity."

To THE EDITOR OF THE WARRINGTON" EXAMINER."

Dear Sir, I have recently been imposed upon, and I wish to issue a scud of warning. Doubtless most of your readers are acquainted with the fact that two gallons of most delicious, thirst-quenching, home-made lemonade can be made in a few minutes from a aid bottle of Eiffel Tower Lemonade. It is, I telieve,sold by most grocers. I have been in the habit of using it with the greatest satisfaction, but going into a 'shop a few days back for sorne,l was recommended to try another make, as being better. I was persuaded to try it, but to my great dis- appointment found it was most inferior. This was most annoying, as I wanted it for a party. I have since ascertained that a larger profit can be made by the trader by supplying an imitation instead of the genuine article. Thinking others might be imposed upon in the same way, I thought I ought to warn your readers not to be put off with inferior makes when they ask for the genuine article. I am, Yours faithfully, A READER.

NOTES ON -GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket. Warrington had Northern for their opponents on S and, all things considered, acquitted them- sealtvner3dacYreclitably in bringing about a draw slightly in their favour. Northern sent down a strong eleven whilst the home team was minus the assistance of E A Connor and Dr. Smith, two valuable members. They however had secured the services of W M Siloock. The 'Northerners had first turn at the wicket and were all out for the respectable total of 199. Warrington had complied 159 for five wickets when play was brought to a doe°.

--- G Holt and W E Stacey were the main support of the visitors as far as batting was concerned. Both players hit out freely, and singularly enough each made four boundary strokes. Altogether Holt put together 62, whilst Stacey placed 53 to his credit.

----- The Rev J Burroug,h had the misfortune to be hurt after knock ng up 8 runs, and was obliged co retire. Airey. Boucher and Ellsworth were in capital form, and piled on 17, 14 and 16 respectively.

--- Warrington had two hours in which to save the match and they took full advantage °tithe opportunity'afforded. J Callon was in grand form and had rattled up 67 when he was bowled by Shutt: He succee3ed in hitting eight lours, and there was not a faulty stroke in the whole of his innings. ‘74 Pochnore, Gibbon and J W Bibby also accomplirthed some very acceptable work, scoring 29, 25, atm 27 respectively.

-- The second eleven' encounter with Northern also ended in • draw. but in this case it cannot be said that the draw was iu favour of Warrington. Northern declared their innings after putting together 236 for 7 wickets. and Warrington had knocked up 87 for 9 wickets when play ceased for the day. Plinston was ;he top scorer for the visitors his innings being productive of 30 not out.

Cycling. A case of considerable interest to members of Volun— teer Cycling Corps was beard at Eccles on Monday. It appears that Ernest Warburton of Weaste, with four other members of the cycling corps of the CI) V .B.M.R. on the 3rd inst rode through Eccles on their bicycles at ten ten p m• Warburton had no lamp, but rode in the milt of the other four who had lights. On Monday he was charged at Eccles with riding without a light, and clairn,d he was entitled to do so, being a Volunteer on .+uty. He cons not in uniform. Captain Evans, ad- jutant, giving evidence in behalf of the defendant, said the men were on duty from the time they left their holies until they returned. It was not necessary for them Ito he in uaiform: The defendant produced his drill bosk. and pointed out that the case Came under the heading of " night Marches." The case was dis- missed without costs,

THE WAR. Arthur H. Grosfield.

EL'.] Sir,—l wish in the first piece to Malik you for } our courtesy for publishing in last Saturday's" Examiner" and offering spacrein your issues lot next Saturday and Saturday week, letters oci the war of really inordinate length. Some Liberal friends of mine will not assent to my conclosiens, but whatever the inferences we draw from them we shall all agree as to the importance of getting at the true facts of the war. Whether they point to the war as having its origin Ia the relief of oppressed Ditimiders, the defence of English and Im- perial iatereste,and the resistance to a Pan-African Con- spiracy ; or whether the inference is th it originally it was engineered by the influence and machioations of a few powerial and unscrupulous men in Sou-iii itrica, let us be clear about our evidence. We shall gain noth- ing by living in a fool's paradise or playing the ostrich and hiding our heads in the sands (of Afric4.) "MY COUNTRY RIGHT OR WRONG" is a sound patriotic doctrine if the question is one of support:, sympathy and unlimited backing, to an army in the field ; and what man or party of any account,hes withheld either ? But when it is broadly hinted that this doctripe coyer a Government's policy- as well and should stifle discusision, then Liberals Bey "No." Bad as things are they would have teen worse If the Govern- ment's policy had not been checked by Liberal criticism. That checked if it did not put an end to a system ot farm burning which was admittedly a mistake ; it stopped the practice of giving half rations to the wives of fighting burghers ; and in these and other particulars bee mended the Government's methodsof carryineon the war. .In this matter history is repeating itself. During the Crimean war John Bright was hustled in the streets of Manchester be- cause he condemned the war policy, and I know—for my father was present when he made the re- markthat Bright said he felt it was almost hopeless to address meetings in time of war. It took fifty years before a Conservative Premier told us thee we put our money on the wrong horse in that war. It will not take five years to convince us that we have backed a worse policy—a poliay soiled with the trail of finance—this time. , In the, American war, which bears, I won't say an ominous but in some re;pects a striking and significant parallel to the present conflict, Fox But e and Chat- ham, who had the courage of their opinions, condemned the Tory podoy of Lord . North's Government. Were they "on their oquatryeeside " or against it ? Every- body knows that up to a late period of the war a settle- ment might have been made and would have been made had Lord Chathana been in power. But the majority of the nation, biased:in their judgment by the same appeals t o the instincts of " man the fighting animal," of which we have had BO much of late, and misled by the wer fever, were against him the Lb ol r ndEd :of nop rri ter hee dhraodvheita. way, and Americansand by g n a to insisting satiirnernoea a policynd d e dealt to f the heaviest blow it has ever received. Jingoes triedfar-sightednp Press s. thnoe racket teal statesmen eanswedi discussion takni :ban: t just te hn fresh the o as Liberal ,rr in the eighteenth century accused our wisest and most ly tsh message om Bothafo r i nwdaesp e sentn d - F section—r iet recent demonstrations of the m cwee: —were Therresponsible fact itbhl fort th not in June but as far back as February 28th ha; cut the ground from under those who would fain start this Cry afresh. ms anyone: suppose that if the Liberals in this country were silenced to-morrow it would have any aporecieble effect on the Boem in the field? Probably very little, and as likely as not It right help to Make cryatt they texny:g wasrne party—or the et raised tryingenemi nye oa tndi Idea them one degree more deseerate. Because it must not te forgotten that,having in so many instances destroyed the family ties, the homes and thestake in the country of the burghers, they are now despe.ateassFvmeraoentmihn.sotirhslotetuevmdgehaorlyyyn be replied that as they are bent on fighting " for their own Independence" the chance of a settlement is hope- less. Are we not rather beating about tße bush over this word "Independence ?" Canada calls herself prac- ticallyabo,commencement mim hesitate deeplye efinnnetdi oenemp loyalieesnne du eos nmsf theirt terms ehamirennmbddwesety.pwernethEnt ehidt snnee ennKceßEvomer rptsi ee I h. e iet-ch eYaeett madeßotha it quite clear as • preliminary that incorporation in the Empire was a sine pea non to any settlement. What seems plain enough is tnat if any settlement is to be made we shall need to guarantee the Boers—not in the dim and distant future, but in some specified time—a full measure of their own self-government under the English flag. I will not write further at the moment on this matter of terms beyond saying that it is by no means certain that if one of our finest diplomats and statesmen, a man like Lord Dufferin, who would not be distrusted by the Dutch and the Boers, were empowered to offer just and reasonable terms they would be refused. After all the Boers know what the terrible alternative of "fighting to a finish" means to them, One sometimes wonders if the Tory papers like the " Daily Mail," that so vehe- mently advocate it, consider also what it will mean to or, and the desperate things which desperate men will do before " the finish " comes, If one points out to Conservatives that the Boers may prefer to go on:fighting to the death rather than accept unconditional surrender, as often as not one is told in reply that the further we exterminate them the better. To say nothing more it is just as well to remember that the greater part of the 'Transvaal will either have to be populated by Dutch farmers or remain a famine stricken desert. Settle and ; ceitivat- it English farmers will not—we know that from the best authorities on the spot. Surely,therefore, terms in place of force should be the final remedy and means of terminating the war. In the letter you published last week I have attempted to describe in rough outline what the war means, on the one band for those who pay for and criticise it at home on the other, for those who suffer it on the spot. It will not improve the political conditions of South Africa. It has burdened our finances. It has cost us tens of thousands of lives. It has involved our own soldiers in our mis-managed hospitals, and the Boer families in the mis-managed csmes in a degree of ERisery that beggars description. It has been one long disaster for both races, it will remain in history a mere , record of uncornpeneated suffering. This is now more and more widely recognised and opinion has changed since the days when so many people at home wished for war, and so many in South Africa laid themselves out to provoke it, while Downing-street went lightly into the contest as though to avoid it were not worth an effort. So recently as lest 0 eober Mr Chamberlain boasted that if he were Its author it would be" a feather in his cap." Even he would not say that .to-day. A month before it began Sir Alfred Milner telegraphedthat British South Africa was prepared for "extreme measures." The President of the South African Lea,gne, the " Loyalist " organisa- tion, even boasted in a public speech at Capetown, that his body might " claim the credit of brineing the war about." For months before it came the English papers in South Africa advocated war as the only remedy. The " Times" when negotiations began to take a fatal turn actually congratulated itself upon onr narrow mane] from peace. The "Saturday Review " I remember took :the same line, and no doubt if they were looked up, we should find a great number of Conservative papers say- ing much the same. All this is forgotten to-day. Even thick and thin supporters of the Government's policy prefer to throw the responsibility for the disastrous algid the cosmopolitan struggle on the Boers, Well, In a sense no doubt the Boers are largely to blame. In the first place, if Mr Kruger had been more tactful and nWneelifizmann lcersoswsdus oPnictreaendinwigihtthhgarveaetevar success. Probably men like Joubert and Louie Botha would have done better. In the second place, be took the fatal step of declaring war. But even that impossible ultimatum which silenced the peace ratty in this country does not make the Transvaal the real aggressor; if, that is, we con- sider the course of events that immediately preceded war. The first thing to ob;erve is that the popular notion that we had the choice between fighting or "knuckling under to Kruger" is an entire mistake. Because he had in fact yielded. as Mr Caamberlain has confessed, " nine-tenths "of our demands. About the other tenth there was a rniaunderstandines and the negotiations came to a staodstile and here it was that, as Mr Morley told the country at the time, Mr Chamberlain made matters so much worse by raising bis terms during the negotiations. Because having set out by demanding the franchise, when this was on the point of settlement, he proceeded to drag in other matters. That:was bid : Further negotiations followed until on September 22nd, Mr Chamberlain announced that our Government would" formulate its owo proposals for a final settlement." The proposals were actually formu- lated by the Cabinet. What they were we do not know, but clearly this dispatio, which was promised (but never sent) was of the nature of an ultimatum. Why was It net sent ? Our military preparations were incomplete, Thr Boere were kept waiting for almost three weeks in igeorence of our proposals. But troops were hurried out to the Cape and drafted from India to Natal. Our forces which had been concentrated in Ladysmith were moved north on September 25 h to Dundee; close to the Trans- Vaal frontier. It Was this march which, as the "Times' baa admitted, in a letter from its special correspondent (published 18th November) induced Mr Kruger to think , about issuing an ultimatum. But even after this clear hint Mr Kruger hesitated. He clearly did not wish to declare war without seeing what our final prrposals really were. On 07,tober 2nd, the Boer Government telegraphed to enqaire whether the dispatch promised on September 22nd WaS not yet ready. Everything hung upon this momentous document. The weeks went past, and still it was delayed, and still our military prepar aions cnntinued. What inference could the Boers arrive at when Mr Cnamberlain merely telegraphed a curt reply to the effect that his de.patch WS9 not yet ready. On thei following day another step was taken which sth. further showed Mr Chamberlain's hand. A semi official announcement was publisnel on 0 ;tober 3rd in all car papers to the tf!ct that Parliament would be

i !misery

summoned on October 17th. It was generally assumed that this extraordinary session was a prelude to the calling out of the treserves, the voting of military supplies and an eventual declaration of war. Meantime President Steyn was eagerly offering his mediation, and the leader of the Africander Band, Mr Jan Hofmeyr strained every nerve to save the situation even at the eleventh hour. A final provocation .rendered their efforts futile. On October 7th our Government issued a proclamation definitely calling out the reserves and summoning the dreaded meeting of Parliament. Then —and not till then—the catastrophe happened. Two days later (October 9th) the Boers issued their ultima- tum. It was s fatal step, a foolish step, but what could we expect. We had convinced the Boers that we in- tended war. Were they to wait inactive while we chose our ow u time for the conflict? Were they to quietly at still while- we assembled our troops at their 000 r? The wonder rather is that they delayed until we had fetched our regiments from India. It was our Government which really made the first appeal to arms by its menacing movements in Natal. As Bacon has said, "There, is, no question but a just fear of an .imminent danger, though there be no blow given, is a lawful cause of war.'

===== 0002601_19010719_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
THB NIOGOTL&TION&_

• But the war, it is constantly said, is due to the fact that President Kruger would not give way to our just demand. Our demand was from the time of the I Bloemfontein Conference onwards that the way should I be smoothed for those Uitlanders who wished Co divest themselves of their Britials Citizenship, to do so by • becoming burghers after having resided in the Trans- ; vaal for five years. And Mr Kruger did give way not , very willingly perhaps, but still gradually and not very , • slowly he did give way. Our demands were formulated in May. By July he had offered a seven years' retros- pective franchise free from conditions. By August he • had gone still further. On August 22nd he offered the five years' retrospective franchise for which we had asked ; eight new seats in the Volksraad for the Uitlanders giving the Rand ten members in a Chamber of thirzy six (mere than we had demanded) ; equal rights in the election of the President, and as to details he welcomed friendly suggestions. His conditions in making them concessions were, (Ist) that the present intervention should not constitute a precedent for future interference in the internal affairs of the Republic,(2nd) that controversy regarding the suzerainty should be tacitly dropped, (3rd) that arbitration, from which foreigners were to be excluded, should be recognised in future disputes. Surely these were reasonable terms. The proposals were what we had demanded. The conditions were not novel. We had just come from the Hague Conference after pledging ourselves to arbitrate with China, Servia, Turkey, &c,, why should we refuse to arbitrate in future with a civilised power like the Transvaal, especially as the proposed court was to exclude foreigners ? As to non-interference WP had pledged ourselves to this in the convention of 1881 and 1884. . • The stipulation about suzerainty was the stumbling block, but as we possessed the reality why fight for the name,especially as we abandoned our claim,to it in 1884. As Lord Salisbury said (October 19, 1899,) in the House of Lords," in order to get that hateful word out of his convention he (Mr Kruger) made considerable territorial and other sacrifices." As recently as the 31st December 1895, Mr Chamberlain him- self in a despatch to the Chartered Company referred to the Transvaal as a "foreign state with which Her Msjesty is at peace and in treaty realtions." It was not until 1897 that he revived our claim to the suzerainty. The question is really a legal one, a ques- tion susceptible of documentary proof, and it is pernaps • worth while to quote the opinion of Sir Edward Clarke, —as we alliknow a great lawyer and a staunch Conserve- ; tive,—on the matter. HA says,"For any British Minister, . since 1884, to assert that this country had a suzerainty over the Transvaal was not only a statement made in defiance of fact hut also a breach of national faith." (19th October 1899. House of Commons).

THE REAL CONSPIRACY. Why then did not Mr Krugm's offer of 22nd August put an end to the dispute? He gave us all we professed to want, and in return asked only for a confirmation of the status which we had granted in 1884 to the Trans- vaal by a convention which made it a sovereign state subject only to our right to veto its treaties with foreign powers. What answer did Mr Chamberlain give? Immediately on receiving this despatch (26th August) he stsod on the front steps of his villa at Elighbury and made the most violent speech he had yet delivered. He complained that Mr Kruger " procrastinates in his replies," " dribbles out reforms like water from a squeersd sponge," and "accompanies his .offers with conditions which he knows to be impossible." He then announced that the l• sands were running down in the glass," and concluded by threatening thatif Mr Kruger did not yield very quickly we should not be content 66 with what we have already offered." These amiable phrases were telegraphed oct to Pretoria. What impres- sion could they possibly produce upon the suspicious old President who had once before seen his Republic annexed by an English High Commissioner? That speech meant that Mr Chamberlain would neaten and bully, that he would not hear Of compromise that there Could be no give and take, that there was no finality in our demands, that it was useless for the Boers to make concessions. The despatch which followed the speech was more carefully worded, but it was utterly ambiguous. Mr Chamberlain says be meant it as a " partial acceptance." Mr Kruger took it sea refusal. No doubt he interpreted the obscure wording of the despatch by the plain language of:the speech. At all events, Mr Chamberlain refused Mr Kruger's not unreas nable conditions. That was the last real attempt at negotiation. Two weeks later Mr Chamberlain announced that we were preparing an ultimatano—an ultimatum which was delayed by the backwardness of our military preparations No, the real question at issue was not the grievances of the I llitlanders,nor the five years' franchise. The" Loyalist" party in South Afrina was determined to overthrow the Republic. On the 29th August 1899 the Johannesburg "Leader," a paper founded to foment this agitation by Messrs Belt and Eckstein, wrote in its leading article under the very eyes of the Pretoria Government :

- _ " The abolition of the Republic is not the end whim we anticipated and hoped for; ; yet we fully

_ recognise the necessity for the step in-view of the hopeless attitude of this Government." That was what Mr Rhodes was really driving at. As in 1895 at the time of the Jameson Raid,so in 1899 the Capitalists and their dupes had determined to make an end of the independence of the Transvaal. No con- cessions would have availed, and the Boers at last made up their mind to face the facts. Sir Alfred Milner had heartily entered into the spirit of this little game. He has admitted it frankly. At a farewell banquet at Cape Town on May 7th 1901 he said, •• Flinching from no sacrifice and turning a deaf ear to people whose endeavour Was ever tending to confine and smother the one cardinal point in a mass of side issues, the British people had gone straight upon the way on which they bad set out from the first,to make an end of the business once and for all, to make South Africa one country under one flag and with one system of law and government." That then is the blunt truth at last. The franchise was a mere "side issue," the "cardinal point" the real aim of the"lovalist" policy was to destroy the Republics for ever. And yet we pretend that the Transvaal was the aggressor.

THE CULPABLE EMBRY. Looking back over the course of these negotiations it is indeed impossible to resist the impression that Ur Chamberlain went to work hastily, angrily and hotly. It is clear enough now that misunderstanding after mis- understanding helped to wreck them which a little patience and tact would have smoothed over. We know from the letters of Messrs Fischer, Merriman and Chief IJustice de Villiers that the Dutch of Cape Colony and ithe Free State were all the while urging Mr Kruger to ' show a tractable disposition. But Sir A Milner made no attempt to use their good offices. He was throughout on the worst terms with his own Cabinet, he suppressed Itheir memorials against *ar, while he quoted and em phasised the violent leading articles in the papers con- trolled by Mr Rhodep and his financial associates. Of Mr Cnanaberlain's negotiations, Sir Edward Grey, has said they were not "diplomacy at all." Surely that admission carries with it a severe condemnation, If the Jameson Raiders bad been punished as they de- served, if the Colonial office bad cared to clear itself from the suspicion of complicity in that erimin a adventure, if the negotiations had been carried out with temper and tact, who can say that the war would have Cottle about? There was no need for haste. The Ilitlanders were making money unmolested when the negotiations began. Life and property were perfectly safe. They could well have waited another three or six or even twelve months for their votes. That was the view which We all took when the question WAS first raised in the spring of 1899. On March 20th in the House of Com- mons Mr Chamberlain himself ridiculed the incitements of Sir Ellis Ashmead Bartlett and laughingly inquired whether that hot beaded patriot" expected us to go to war with the Transvaal." He reviewed all the stock grievances—the Edgar case, the Dynamite monopoly, tne demand for the franchise, the general unrest—and Yet his conclusion was that interference would be neither "dignified "nor " expedient." and he wound up with the announcement that be" did not intend to take any very strong action." His own party took the same view. The " Times " for example said next morning "Inter- ference is inadvisable. . • . . Such a policy wculd not carry public opinion in this country with it. •. . The Boers must be allowed still to go their own way. Time is not on their side." If interferenee was inexpedient in March, what made war necessary in October ? If time was on our side in the Spring. what i compelled us to force the pace n the Autumn ? The answer is that the clique of financiers who controlled the South African Press had resolved on war. They obtained the ear of the High Commissioner ;it was in their name that be called for" extreme measures." Six months of heated telegrams, violent speeches and manu- factured outrages served to mislead opinion in this country and stirred up the black cauldron of hatrtd and misery in South Africa. London, July Bth, 1901.

METHODISM AT STOCKTON

HEATH.

A New Chapel to be Erected.

Laying of Memorial Stones. Saturday last witnessed an important event in the history of Independent Methodism, when the ceremony of laying memorial stones on the site of the new Provid- ence Chapel to be erected at Stockton Heath, was performed. The site of the new chapel is at the end of Chapel-lane, and it is proposed to spend £1,200 upon the erection. About the year 1806 the famous American Evangelist,Loreize Dow,visited Eagiand,and conducted a Mission in Friars Green Chapel. Warrington. In com- pany with the late Mr Peter Pnillips he came out to Stockton Heath, and seeing a barn near to the site of the proposed new chapel, expressed a desire to preach I there. A series of services were held. As a result, a society was formed, and services were conducted there I until 1827 when the present chapel and school pre- mises were erected. The Sunday school opened in the barn is believed to be the first in this part et Lancashire' or Cheshire. However that may be, the Sunday school has continued its work without a break up to the present day. At this barn chapel, the first total abstin- ence society in England, it is claimed, was formed. Consequent upon the growth of the neighbourhood, the increased number of scholars bas made it necessary to use the whole of the premises for Sunday school work. It nely be added that all the labour of tbe ministry, the chapel and the school is voluntary, and that,though the members of the society belong mostly to the labouring class, the present chepel and schcol are free from debt. A large assembly gathered to witness Saturday's pro- ceedings. The Brick-street Independent Methodist Temperance Band was in attendance and played selections of music and also accompanied in the singing of the hymns. Mr R. B. Woods presided and was accompanied on an improvised pleform over the foundation by a number of prominent ladies and gentlemen connected with the society. After devotional exercises and the singing of a hymn, The Chairman delivered a brief address in which be referred to the early history of the movement. After extending a hearty greeting to all present, he said : We are able to fix the date of the commencement of our society with perfect accuracy. In December, 1805, Lorenzo Dow end his wife left America on a missionary tour to England. In the spring of 1806, Peter Phillips of Warrington was on business in Liverpool, and while there he attended a service conducted by the American Evangelist. He was so impressed with the preacher's power and disinterestedness that he invited him to visit Warrington. Mr Dow says he went to Warrington, found a pious people worshipping in a small chapel (Friars Green) called Quaker Methodists, because they are so simple using plain language, and holding class meetings. The Evangelist conducted service in the chapel two and three times a day,and God so owned the labours, that 40 members were added to the Church. On May 30th, 1806, about the time of the death of his only daughter, whose remains were laid to rest in Friars Green chapel yard, he received an invitation to preach in the barn, then standing near this site, and the work was so blessed of God, that the Warrington preachers of this denomination continued to visit the People and formed the Church, which for the past 95 years has continued the work of the Freest of the Free Churches. In testimony of the value of the work done by Mr Dow, at Warrington, Lowton, Lymm, and this place an address was presented to him this address was. signed by R Harrison. R Mills, tV Mo , Gullets, Peter Phillips, G Britnelow (relatives of Mr Mills I and Mr G Brimelow we have with us to day); and over 100 other members testified to good received. The evi- dence of the founding of the Sunday school is not quite so clear as to date. But it is certain the father of Mrs Brirnelow, who is to lay the first stone to day, was a scholar in this school about the years 1812-14. And a succession of faithful men and women have continued this beneficent work, until it is generally acknowledged that all the old families of the district have been in- fluenced for good by this auxiliary • In March, 1830 three years after the erection of the present chapel, Mr' G H Birkett of Dublin, and Mr W Wood of Manchester' members of the Society of Friends visited Warrington,: They were deeply interested in the temperance ques tion, and for several days made every effort to secure a room for the purpose of lecturing on this subject. As no room was available, Mr T Eaton offered them Providence Chapel for this purpose. On April 4th, 1830, the following pledge was taken and a society formed :—" We whose names are subscribed believing that intemperance with its attendant evils, are promoted by the prevailing opinions and eractices of society with regard to the use of intoxicating liquors, and that decided means are called for, resolve to abstain from the use of inebriating liquors ourselves, and dis- suade others from using them, and by all proper means to discountenance the cause and practice of intemper- ance." This is believed to be tie first total abstinence pledge. And in 1834 a former scholar of the Sunday School, Mr R Mee, formed a society in Warrington, with one pledge of total abstinence. This society is believed to be the oldest of existing societies, and emanated I from Providence Chapel. From its inception until now this church has continued to interest herself in the ' moral, spiritual and educational interest of the people. And as the rural aspect of the locality passes lute a residential centre, our Sunday School bag become too small for the scholars sent to us, and our chapel accom- modation quite unequal to the demands. We are, there- fore, compelled to build this place, and use the whole of the older premises for Sunday, echool, and educational work. We believe thie same faithfulness and disinter- estedness in service will characterise the men and women upon whose shoulders will be the burden of the future work, and we are fully satisfied that the glory of the latter house will exceed the former, because the mis- sionary spirit is strong in the churoh,and the educational advantages of our children are consecrated to the , Master's service. (Applause). The memorial stones were then laid in the following order :—Presentation of trowel to Mrs W Brimelow, by •Mr Turner Hartley ; firat stone laid by Mrs Brimelow, of Bolton. Presentation of trowel to Mns J by Mr W Price: second stone laid by Mrs J Mounfield. of Stockton Heath. Presentation of trowel to Mrs T Hartley, by Mr J S Riley; third stone laid by Mrs T Hartley, of Colne. Presentation of trowel to Mrs R Henshall, by Mr T Backhouse ; fourth stone laid by Mrs Henshall, Warrington. Presentation of trowel to Mr J Crumblehulme, by Mr Joseph Mounfield; fifth stone laid by Mr Crumblehulme (President of the Connexion). ,Presentation of trowel to Mr J W Woods, by Mrs Bray- haw ; sixth stone laid by Mr J W Woods, Birmingham. Presentation of trowel to Mr R Lee. by Mrs W Shaw ; seventh stone laid by Mr Richard Lee, of Wigan. Pre- sentation of trowel to Mr James Ince, by Mrs R B Woods; eighth stone laid by Mr James Ice, of Lovrton. Congratulatory speeches were made, and the hope was generally expressed that the erection of the chapel would be attended by the good results anticipated by the founders. Several hymns were sung during the pro- ceedings and a collection was taken. Public Meeting

A well attended public meeting was held in the even- - log, a large marquee having been erected for the pur- pose in a field adjoining the site of the new chapel. Mr Joseph Mounfield occupied the chair and amongst others present were Messrs J Crumblehulme (president of the connexion), W Brimelow (Bolton), R Henshall, (president of the circuit), R B Woods J Mort and others. It the outset the Chairman said Mr Brayshaw had just handed him a paper which showed a result with which they would be perfectly satiefied. Their cash in the bank amounted to £76 185 9d, while the collections and donations in the afternoon amounted to the excel- lent sum of £l3B. (Applause.) That was a record collection in connection with the Providence Chapel. (Hear, hear, and applause). He hoped that smile time they would be able to do even better. They were extremely pleased with what had been done that day ;It gave them very great encouragement to receive such sympathy. They had heard that afternoon the history of their chapel. Those who be, rd it would be satisfied that they had reason to be proud of such a record. They had in it an encouragement to go on. If they let the present opportunity slip they might have to wait a long time tor it to come again. (Hear, hear.) It was neces- sary for them to feel their own needs and Sr quirements. The very fact that they had over 80 Sunday school scholars to be taught in:a room 20 feet by 14 feet and barely eight feet high showed that they badly needed more accommodation. They bad been compelled to use the chapel for Sunday school purposes. a thine which was very undesirable. More room was also required in the chapel. On the previous Sunday night the building was quite full. He had also known when they had had to put torms down the aisks during ordinary services. There were not only their own requirements to be con- sidered, but there were the requirements of the village. They bad to keep up with the times or sink. They were now making a bold move to take a foremost plan among the Nonconformist chapels in the village. He was pleased to see the noble way in which their friends in the Church of England had come forward to help them. They bad shown their sympathy by practical help. He thanked the members of the building and chapel committees, and prayed that God's blessing would rest upon the effort in which they were engaged.

KEEPS THE

tn the new chapel they would bare seating annum= dr lion for 280 people, and would be able to seat over 300

f occasion required it. (Applause.) Mr John Crumblehukce, president of the Connexion, said he was pleased to be present to witness the stone laying of a new church in that locality. Be quite agreed with the Chairman that that step had not been taken any too sox]. He thought that it might have been taken earlier. He congratulated the Stockton Heath church people upou the sympathy and pracical help they bad shown. He believed that the best part of Methodism bad been reared in the country N!liages and he found that in this part of the country their church was realiz- ing its position and responsibilities and bad made up its mind that it would be among the most aggressive sections of the Connexion. The Connexion meant to be aggressive, Christianity could not be stationary. If a man or a woman was a Christian they most grow, and

iritia -Church was to be what Goa' through Christ intended it to be it must grow, and progress. What were the essentials of success in a Christian ? First, loyalty to Christ ; second, loyalty to the leaders of the church, and third, love towards one another. Those three Essentials practised in any church would certainly bring about success. The Chairman said they had raisod -£l3O and that meant practically that they bad got with the cash in hand, a sixth of what they wanted. He asked them to rally round the people there, and to give them all the help ;they could. In conclusion Mr Crumblehulrne said he should like to see the day when the Chairman would lay the foundation stone of chapel number two. (Ap- plause.) Mr W Brimelow, of Bolton, gave a brief history of the church, and mentioned in connection with its foundation and progress such well-known names as Lorenzo Dow, Thomas Heaton. William Yates, George Peck, Thomas Eyes, and others. After the choir had reodered the hymn "Fading away." Mr Robert Elensball addressed the meeting, and spoke particularly upon spiritual work before the church in the future. The anthem" Onward Christian soldiers was then sung by the choir. Mr R B Woods proposed that the thanks of the assembly be tendered to all those who had so liberally and nobly rendered their services. Mr James Mort seconded and the resolution was carried with applause. The Chiirman having returned thanks the meeting concluded with the singing of the Doxology. It was explained during the proceedings that two of the gentlemen (Messrs J Worthington and G Hunter) announced to address the meeting were unavoidably absent.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

The meeting of this society on Sunday afternoon was presided over by Mr. William Light. In the course of a brief address, the Chairman made an earnest appeal to the men to declare themselves on the Lord's side. Mr G F Heaps, of Manchester. delivered the address. and spoke upon the value of seizing opportunities.—The President(Rev John Yonge)announced that two members were leaving them for distant shores. Mr Elijah Lunt was proceeding to Australia, and Mr Buckley, of the St. John Ambulance Brigade, had just returned to South Africa for hospital work* Re was sure they would follow both members with interest in their new spheres of life. —An organ solo was contributed by Mr W H Payton, Mus. Bac. Mr Webb and Mr Whitfield also took part in he service.
