The Evening THE two-edged nature of School Minute. the thrust which the Govern- ment has made in the vitals 01 the School Board system of education, by means Of the minute issued by the Board of E iucation Or the regulation of the evening continuation Schools, was well exposed by the debate in the Rouse of Lords on Monday afternoon. Applying the keen edge of restriction to age, the Board had severed the vast majority of the pupils from the evening schools of the School Board, with the result, as blandly expressed in the letter of Sin ,3.°1:1N GORST'S secretary, that the schools cannot long survive the stroke. With the other edge an Outlet has been opened for a complete subversion 01 the principles of the Education Act of 1870, so gross as to be declared illegal by both LORD BRAY and EARL SPENCER, and apparently on very good rinthority. In their eagerness to provide a sub Vention for the Voluntary schools the Board of Zducation have gone the length of applying the grant which they have withdrawn from School Board pupils above fifteen to pupils of all ages in anY other kind of school. Now, it was a principle Clearly established by the Education Act of 1870 that the Parliamentary grant was to be given only to schools which came under the designation of public elementary schools, and these Schools were required to fulfil certain conditions 813ecified by the Act. But the Government, while Scrupulously careful about preserving the law to Its letter, and even beyond it, so far as it can be held to apply adversely to Board schools, have no Filleh scruple when the schools of their friends °inside of the School Boards are concerned. Hence it is found that the new minute makes the grant applicable to outside schools of any description, Without any reference to the original designation °1 public elementary schools. It may have been the result of an oversight; but EARL Srexcen,by quoting the wordsof the Appropriation Act,which 'Were altered in 1900, appears to indicate that the Qovernmeat had previously been contemplating their present action and preparing the way for it. It ia I,t - well-known that the restrictive action of the —c'ard of Education on the School Board work is nut a thing of yesterday, and that they did not Wait for the Cockerton judgment for initiating their campaign of restriction and demolition. It is therefore quite reasonable to suppose that the words of the Appropriation Act, which now bear n. general relation to salaries and expenses of the Board of Education, and of various establishments and authorities in connection therewith, and sun- dry grants, were adopted in full view of the cam- Ysign which was then determined on, and is now uerng carried out. As Lord PORTSMOUTH said on Ronday,the real injustice of the new continuation school minute is that it proposes one scheme for Voluntary schools and another for Board schools. Itl3 indeed difficult, as his lordship said,to under- stand the prejudice in the minds of the Board of tducation regarding the Board schools. The great bulk of them have done goed work. Even kr BALFOUR has admitted that 99 per cent of them might be carried on without overlapping; t4erl yet the only justification Ministers can find their action consists of party gibes ar d alleged Nilts of the most trumpery nature. The BISHOP ii4tIEREFORD bore noble testimony to the excel- 40t work that has been done under combined Saanagernent at Manchester, where sectarian jealousies have been laid aside with the most beneficent results, under Mr ACLAND'S code. Sir /LLIAM HART DYKE, Major BA.NEs of South West Ram, and other Unionists who know and appreciate the good work done by School Boards in the cause of national education, have borne testimony which is a sufficient answer to all the silly and transparent taunts of interested members of the Government. If the Government deter- mine to 'carry through their campaign on the present lines,it will not be without ample warning of its mischievous results.

There is said to be a movement on the General Inedical Council for a yet stricter interpretation of the rule which forbids advertisement by medical Practitioners. It is not sufficient that the surgeon Or Physician should be forbidden the use of the advertisement columns of the newspapers which is allowed to the banker, the musician, and even in ,lsrlroe degree to the divine. He must in the future abstain from permitting portraits of him- !elf to appear in the papers on occasions on which, or instance, he has been a leading actor in some great public ceremony, and he must not on any account allow biographies of himself to appear, Whether or not they mention his record as a prac- titioner or his qualifications. The advocates of this new code of professional honour do not seem to see the ridiculous futility of such a proposal. If it is to be logically carried out, the proprietors of every illustrated paper would have to erase,say, from the view of a stone-laying the figure of a surgeon who happened to be interested in the matter, or from a group of town councillors they would have to strike out any physician who may be serving his fellow-townsmen as their elected representative. Nay, a medical man, on present- ing himself as a candidate for the choice of the ratepayers, must expect to see in the papers Counterfeit presentments and appreciative bio- Rrapkies of his rivals, and know that any refer- !Mee to himself would bring dire penalties upon his head. But cannot the General Medical Council Ro further? Why not forbid polished door- Plates, which are certainly an advertisement in their way, and deny successful practitioners those *ell-equipped carriages which advertise the suc- °las, and therefore, presumably, the merit, of their owners?

The tragic death of two young actresses in Eloomesbury illustrates the hardness of the dra- Inatic career in its early stages,even for those whose ' ability gives promise of success. Miss Olga Nether- anle some time ago wrote an article for the benefit = Id aspirants. She told them the conventional idea Of what was required fell far short of the demands. ' -clae popular notion is that an actress must have a Pretty face, a good figure, an emotional nature, ; and a voice that is capable of expressing emotions In all their varying shades. These things are, nowever, only a small part of what is needed. An actress must have a strong physique, an optimism Which will enable her to bear the buftetings of fortune, and—a good digestion. In her early .days = she is poorly paid for the work she has to do, and in addition to the fatigue of the performance in Foot of the footlights, the late hours and the tleated atmosphere,there frequently preparation of new pieces to be done in the mornings; and, for most,there is the touring from town to town— Working six days a week and travelling on the gnnday. All the prizes in the profession are the to obtain, and so triumph is greater for Lae women who emerge from the ordeal with fame and a handsome income. But the truth should be nrn k in order that girls who would enter op th car' eer because it seems outwardly so pleasant raaY not suffer a painful disillusionment. The two Vilng ladies who committed suicide in/ Blooms- .' urY had a splenaid pride,which made them object to +I, I t thought of appealing to friends for help. bl_t•if they had had a little more patience, pro- a°l3, their talents would have carried them along. There

has been no week so exciting as this in World of cricket for many seasons. First of all

it has seen the fall of the mighty. The invincible Yorkshiremen who had not sustained one defeat in forty-seven consecutive engagements during the past two seasons were severely defeated on Wed- nesday at Leeds by Sotnersetshire, one of the lowliest of the first-class cricket teams, It was a most remarkable game, for on the first innings Yorkshire had a lead of some 250 runs, which the Somerset men not only wiped off, but compiled the huge total of 630 runs and ultimately won by a majority of 279 runs. This is not half a beating, but a real honest thrashing, and it is also to be remembered that Somersetshire came as near as possible to bringing about a simi- lar result early in the season at Taunton. Tney have.also heavily defeated the sister county team of Lancashire, and t:iree years ago they knocked Surrey out of the championship in the last match of the season and thus left the County Palatine in possession of the laurels. It is a brave eleven that is capta;ned by the popular SM J Woods. This week's games have also seen a first-class match end in a tie—that between the South Africans and Worcester—who each scored 433 runs. On Wednesday, too, Mold issued a reply to the statements on the throwing question by James Phillips. Some of his argu- ments are very penetrative,while in his concluding sentence the Lancashire express simply bowls the ' umpire middle stump. The throwing question is not ended, however, and the variety of opinion in regard to Mold is considerable. Whether majori- ties must rule remains to be seen.

It is not a very dispiriting fact that the War- rington Musical Society is in debt to the tune of £l5O. Societies of this description are so depen- dent on public favour and the humour of the mul- titude that it is not an uncommon or surprising thing for them to show a deficit, and the members as a rule are not greatly alarmed when they do. Of course it would be vastly more encouraging if they paid their way and made a profit. The War- rington Musical Society has had a bad year, but 'then this last twelve months has been a bad time for most organisations dependent on public sup- port, and a better future may reason- ably be looked forward to. There was little despondency at the society's annual meeting held last week, rather, indeed, a strain of cheerfulness for everybody felt that the deficit could easily be wiped off by a special effort. Mr J Parr, the president,made an interesting allusion to the class of concerts given by the society and ven- tured to suggest that the standard was too high. This seems rather a bold idea but there is much to be said in support of it. Theoretically we carniof have too high a class of music,and in the matter of educating the public taste •up to good music the society has done an invaluable work for many years, but the question which arises is : does the society cater for the best paying section of the public? The answer to that question is clearly, Ni! Quite likely the society would not attempt to cater for ' that section, even though it paid, if doing so involved the lowering of the high class of music which the members have learned to look upon as something not only expected from them, but something it is their special object to provide. Yet the advisability or otherwise of departing from the supremely classical occasionally is worth considering. We all know how popular the operas are. Why should not the Warrington Musical Society make an occasional excursion into the domain of Gilbert and Sullivan? Some such ven- ture would probably be attenAed with gratifying financial results, and the society would not have lowered very much the standard of its music.

The Borough Treasurer's abstract of accounts for the County Borough of Warrington has been published a month earlier :this year, and considering that the financial year only ended on the 31st of March last, Mr Fairhurst is to be con- gratulated upon the expeditious manner in which the numerous accounts have been presented to the ratepayers. The accounts form a bulky tome of nearly 200 pages, but it is not one of those produc- tions which is likely to be read from cover to cover at a single sitting. It is crammed with dry facts but nevertheless it contains some interesting in- formation for the sorely taxed ratepayer. An advance of eightpence in the 2 in the rates will probably serve to stimulate a keener in- terest in Corporation expenditure, and by a study of the Borough Treasurer's state- ment, the ratepayer may obtain some information as to how the rates have been spent. The return showing the assessable value and rates levied since 1857 shows clearly the tendency of the rates to advance within recent years. For the first forty years from 1857, the average rates levied have ranged from 5s 81d to 5s 10d in the 2 whereas the average for the past five years has been 6s 1?,-43 in the 2. This advance has taken place notwithstanding the fact that the assess- able value has risen from £173,040 to £212,853 within the past five years. This increase in assessable value is due to a great extent to an increased assessment of existing property so that the increase in the amount of rates paid is con- siderably more than is represented by the averages when compared with the preceding years.

From the aggregate balance sheet we learn that the total assets of the borough now exceed one million sterling, the exact estimate being £1,183,850 4s 11d. The liabilities to be set against this sum are put down at £858,029 19s id. The only accsunt which shows a deficiency is that relating to the electricity de- partment, the deficit amounting to £867 2s 7d. This deficiency is entirely due to bank interest and commission. The liabilities are £44,960 15s lid and up to the end of March last the income was only £457 4s id. This is the result of the first three months' working and can hardly be taken as a criterion of what the actual results will be. When the proposed extensions have been made, the liabilities will approach £60,000 and the undertaking will require very careful handling if it is to make a profit. The tramways undertaking does not enter largely into this year's account, but it will perhaps come as a mild shock to the unsuspecting ratepayer that the preliminary expenses of obtaining the order have amounted to £1,447. The progress of both these municipal undertakings within the next few years will have an important bearing upon the rates,but at the present moment there is no reason for pessimism. The results may even exceed the anticipations of the greatest optimist.

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter. --- Victoria Day, by the bill which is now before Parliament will net interfere with any of the cu tomary national holidays. The object of the measure is to amend the Bank Holidays Act of 1871 and the Holidays Extension Act of 1875 in such way as to make an additional bank holiday to ba called Victoria Dar The sentiment of loyalty which underlies the scheme is admirable, for, while on every hand eff >rts are being made to perpetuate ' the blessed memory of Queen Victoria by concretel memorials, this measure would create a great popu- lar festival in perpetuation of a Sovereign who name must ever be associated with a superb era of British history. Victoria Day, in short means another national or bank boliLlay. So that neither Whitsuntide nor the August holidays are to he dis- placed. It is tolerably clear, though not yet ' explicitly stated in the House of Commons, that Victoria Day will be made to fall between the Whitsuntide and the August Holidays. Either the anniversary of Accession Day or Coronation Day may be the date selected. Both are suitable from the public point of view, because both fall at that season of the year most acceptable for outdoor holiday making. And this will leave unbroken the long spell of work and application lying between , the first Monday of August and Christmas Day.

Members of Friendly S3cieties have a right to feel that they have been badly treated by the House of Lords in the rejection of the Friendly Societies (Outdoor Relief) Bill. One noble lord described it ai being backed, "with 0119 exception," by vote-hunting humanitarians. The result was eo unexpected that it has been received with a feeling of con.ternation among the class directly affected. The argument that appea•ed to influence the peers none of whom can ever have been in a position to dread poverty ia old age, was that this would be introducing a new principle in.° poor-law adminis-

tration, which, of course, it would. This was the great merit of the bill Cheir lordships, however, living in a calm region away from the stress of life as endured by the poor,declined to sanction the change, althoug'a advocated by Lord James of Hereford, Lord Rosebery and others, after having passed through all its stages in the House of Com- mons. There could not be any tangible objection to the measure, which was remarkably brief, and simply proposed to make compulsory hitherto per- missive r-Tulations that in granting poor relief account need not be taken of incomes derived from friendly society allowances up to but not exceed- ing five shillings per week. The wisdom of thus directly punishing thrift is more than questionable.

St Swithin's Day found many people observing the weather anxiou-ly even in this first year of the twentieth century. The forecast for Monday sug- gested showers which, as the day advanced, came not, and those who longed for rain had their hopes in that quarter blighted. The legend of bow the good old Bishop of Winchester prevented his monks interring him in the chancel, and enforced the ful- filment of his wishes by sending heavy rain on July 15th and for forty days after,is now almost forgotten Yet the weather lore that was deducted from the legend, or it; may be gave rise to it, has as many ad- herents now as ever, deTite all that meteorological authorities may say, and have said against it.

Police officers in every part of the provinces are interested in a decision which Judge Waddy has just given, affecting pensions. The Sheffield Watch Committee had refused to take house accommoda- tion and free supply of gas, coal, and water allowed to officers into consideration, in calculating the retiring pensions to which three inspectors were entitled. The inspectors appealed to the quarter sessions, and the recorder, Judge Waddy, pro- nounced in favour of the appellants, whose pensions are consequently increased some £2O a year. It would appear that the Watch Committee bad regarded house rent, and a free supply of light, water and fuel as mere perquisites, or something thrown in as " extras.' But these items invariably constitute a very important consideration in any man's domestic arrangements, and even when "thrown in," are naturally looked upon as part and parcel of the ordinary income.

Dr. Macoamara brought before the House of Commons on Tuesday a matter that has often exci- ted attention in the provinces, but not always so much as it ought to have done. For a long time past municipalities have been hampered in neces sary work by the provisions insisted upon by the Local Government Board with regard to the raising of loans. The first question raised related to the loans of municipalities for the erection of working- class dwellings. Experience proves that the terms upon which municipalities are allowed to borrow check municipalenterprise. It was contended that municipalities ought to build at such cost tha the rents would 301110 within reach of the poorest, and this is obviously true. But the rules of the Local Government Board prevent it. As Dr Macnamara said, "the repayment in the limited number of years now insisted upon made it imperative to charge rents which are prohibitive to the class most in need." It is not the security that is in question ; nor is it the lenders or the borrowers who stand in the way ; but the Local Government Board, which considers it elf the '' trustee for posterity." AS was said in this letter a week or tw I ago, it is not desirable that local authorities should have no check on their borrowing, and should be able to leave the greater part of all loans to be paid by posterity ; but the nature of the security, the permanence of the work to be done, and the moral value of it, are also considerations that might be expected to guide the action of the Local Government Board.

--- Political parties are now actively engaged in ob- taining the claims of lodgers who- are on the old register and entitled to again claim their votes. All lodgers who have been in occupation of their lodg- ings since the 15th of July last year, and are pay- ing not less than four shillings per week, are en- titled to make a claim under this franchise.

Motoring is likely to become still more fashionable by the partiality which the king is displaying for the new vehicle. His Majesty is known to have bought several cars for his personal use, and although he will not, it is said, take any of them with him when proceeding to the Continent a few weeks hence, it is added that he intends to have some cars built for continental use for which they will be reserved. Society will then, of course,want its holiday motors, and things point to increasing prosperity in this comparatively new • industry which has arisen. So that, in spits of all prognos- tications to the contrary, and for weal or for woe to the ordinary pedestrian, the autocar promises to have quite" a future."

Resolutions by Boards of Guardians in various parts of the provinces, regretting the action of the House of Lords in throwing out the Friendly Societies (Outdoor Relief) Bill,are becoming a kind of "order of the day."

Provincial people are, of course, very much in- terested in the debates going on in Parliament relating to the latest education measure. The only strong point made in the House of Commons this week by Sir John Gorst, appears to be in his criti- cism of the' smaller school beards, and here is a curious illustration which cr living up at the pre- sent jancture, must be regarded as striking of its kind. A small Somerestshire village has a school board, of whic4 four out of its five members are churchmen. So this board, in seeking for a master for the board school, decided to stipulate "Church- man preferred," and further decided by three to two that applications should be addressed to the Vicar the Chairman. After this, it must be ad- mitted that it is certainly undesirable the educa- tional area should be so small as it sometimes as.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BT 11. CORRESPONDENT.] The special meeting of the Runcorn Urban Dis- trict Council was for the purpose of transacting important business in connection with the Trans- porter Bridge. Everyone would like to bear of a definite date being fixed for the commencement of building operations. That it cannot be long de- layed is a general opinion and the special meeting of the Council, and the fact that the Widnes Cor- poration Committee have dealt with plans of the approaches at 'the Widnes end of the Bridge goes far to confirm it.

Ihe school teachers of the Runcorn Board Schools are ti be hiphly complimented on the success of their prize scheme, and as I some time ago took exception to the quality of the prizes Riven, I must of necessity show my appreciation of the veryl excellent lot of prizes they offered the children on this last occagion. A good many of them passed through my hands and I have nothing but the highest words of commendation to bestow upon those responsible for this year's selec- tion. Those people who have supported the "prize fund" can accept my assurance that the money has been well-spent.

General regret is expressed at the removal from Runcorn of Superintendent Parson. though there is not an individual in the town who regrets his advancement. He has not been long amongst 113 but in the short time at his disposal he has done much in removing snares and pitfalls which too often proved the undoing of the sailors who fre- quented the port. Inspector Hicks, who has been amongst us for a much longer time, also leaves on the first of August, but it is not generally known that the well-known and genial Constable Carring- ton, whose figure is perhaps the most familiar one in Runcorn, is also on the removal list. He goes, I understand, with his prevent superintendent.

Mr 9 Woodward at the meeting of the Board o Guardians took exception to the manner in which the correspondent to the " Liverpool Daily Post had worked out his figures. The matter arose in' connection with a comparsion made by Mr Wood- ward of the " old " and " new" Dietary Order, gr Woodward stating that there was a difference of £l3O or so in the bills for a fortnight and the corresponding fortnight." Mr Woodward also took the opportunity of pointing out "that the compari- son was not a fair one." but still it would do to show there was a distinct difference in the work- ing of the two orders ; though it was not quite so much as the Master had first thoncht it would be. —The " Post" Correspoodent, however, had ap- parently worked the figure out for the twelve months and represented an additional cost of some £3,000 odd on the year. That this was not so, one was glad to learn. Mr Woodward failed to account for the foundation of the report, and could only attribute it to the" hot weather and the indescrib- able, non-understandable, propensities of the printers devil." One would now like to know the true comparison.

AN UNFORTUNATE RUNCORN CONTRACT.

-- - At the London Bankruptcy Court,on Wednesday, an application was made to Mr Registrar Giffard to grant an order of discharge to Vincent James Barton, iron merchant and contractor, of 15, Wallbrook, London. It appeared that the debtor, who had been in business for many years, in December, 1897, entered into a 'contact with the Lancashire White Lead Com- pany, Runcorn to erect plant and machinery at their works at that place for £13,000, payab:e in shares of the company. The order for the machin- ery was given to Messrs Robert Daglish and Co. engineers, of St Helens, for which £2,315 was to be paid. Litigation arose in connection with the goods supplied with the eventual result that Messrs Diglish recovered judgment for £574 and costs, and instituted the preeent bank- ruptcy proceedings. Mr Hough, senior official re- ceiver, reported that the debts were £2,306, but ' that only creditors for £433 has proved against the estate; the assets had realized £100; that the debtor had previously failed 21 years ago, as the result of a railway com- pany having made default in payment for rolling stock supplied b 3 He hal been in business 40 years, and attributed his failure to the law costs of the action (about £950), to bad debts (.£235). and to his profits some years ago having been insufficient to meet his expenses. The offences alleged were in- suffiaient assets and books,continning to trade with a knowledge of insolvency, and a previous failure. Mr Harper, for the debtor, disputed certain of the findings in the report as to the previous failure il,nd the books, and asked the Registrar, having regard ' to the debtor's age, to grant the discharge subject to judgment being entered for a certain amount. The Registrar found the previous failure not proved, and suspended the discharge for two years.

Bertie Howells, aged seven, of Porter-street,Run- corn (whose father is serving at the front) acci- dently fell into the Bridgewater Canal, whilst play- ing in its vicinity near Halton Crane, on Wednes- day evening. Lucky for him Thomas Horsefield, boatman, was near the spot, and immediately accomplished his rescue, carried him home and brought him to consciousness. This is about the eighth life that Horsefield has been instrumental in saving, and we think that the gallant boatman is deserving of some recognition from the Humane Society. ADULTERATED MILK AT WESTON POINT. At the Runcorn Sessions, on Monday. before Messrs F J Norman and W Timmins,George Mayers, provision dealer, Weston Point, was fined 104 and 244 6d costs for selling milk adulterated with 6 per cent of added water. The case was proved by Mr Albert Timmis, weights and measures inspector. of Chester Castle. For the defence, it was alleged that the milk had not been tampered with in transit from the cow to the customer, and probably the dry weather would account fer its poverty.— Mr Timmis pointed out, however, it was not the absence of the fatty solids but of the non-fatty solids, that the analyst, Dr James Carter-Bell,bssed his report on; when the non-fatty solids were in- sufficient it was proof that something had been put into it

RUNCORN POLICE CHLNGES.

Consequent upon the retirement on pension of Chief Superintendent Large, of Middlewieh and Northwich, Superintendent David Pearson, of Run- corn, has been promoted to first-class superinten- dent and transferred to Middle wicb. Superinten- dent Pearson has had a distinguished career in the Cheshire force. Prior to being appointed superin- tendent al, Runcorn, he was detective inspector at headquarters, Chester, for over six years, and he unravelled many knotty problems in the detection of crime, He will be succeeded at Runcorn by Inspector George Ennion, of Sale, who is promoted to superintendent. Runcorn is also to lose the services of Inspector Hicks, who is transferred to Altrincham. Inspector Alexander, of Altrincham, is transferred to Sale. vice Inspector Ennion. The alterations will be effected as from the let A ugust next.

FIRE AT RUNCORN. On Saturday evening Mr Robert Wright, super- intendent of the Runcorn Fire Brigade, was going towards Halton, when he discovered some hay on fire in a field off Halton-road. He came back into the town and summoned the fire brigade, who pro- ceeded to the spot with all possible haste. The hay which belonged to Mrs Richardson, butcher, of Church-street, Runcorn, had been cut, and a por- tion of it trussed ready for removal. There were four tons of it altogether, an 1 its value was estim- ated at £l5. The hay was practically destroyed or rendered unfit for use, but the damage occasioned thereby is fully covered by insurance. HOLY TRINITY CHURCIT,RIINCORN. In the afternoon a flower service was held,at which a seasonable address was given by the Rev El B Firth. RUNCORN Pluisix Cffuncir. The junior members of the choir had their annua trip on Saturday last, the favoured resort being! Southport. The party, about 30 in number, in charge of the Rev H B Firth and Messrs F Boraston and J left Widnes soon after nine o'clock, arriving at Southport about 10 30. After a stroll through the leading thoroughfares a move was made towards the Temperance Hotel, where an amazing variety of dishes made glad the eyes of the hungry choristers. After a hearty repast the party spent a short time by the shore, and then proceed- ed by train to the Botanic Gardens, Churchtown. For a time the proprietor of the rowing boats and canoes on the lake was unusually active, but later on the lake was deserted for the pleasures of the museum, conservatory, swings, &c., and about 4 p m the tram was again requisitioned. Tea was subsequently served at ',IIEI Temperance Hotel. The time for assembling at the station came all too quickly, but on the way home general expressions of satisfaction were heard on all sides.

-- FORTUNE-TETLING AT RUNCORN. At Runcorn, on Thursday, before Messrs F J Nor- man and R H Posnett, Mary Smith, alias Gipsy Nell. a smart-looking young woman of eighteen years, was charged with professing to tell the for- tune of,Jane Yates, wife of a chemical worker. The prisoner told Mrs Yates she would show her some- thing to her advantage, and asked for the largest piece of silver in the house. Mrs Yates produced a 'crown, which prisoner pinned in a piece of brown paper, and placed in a tuhibler half-filled with water. Prisoner then breathed in the glass and Mrs Yates saw smoke arise. Prisoner said it would be necessary to place the silver on her planet-glass at home, and she pocketed the money and went sway. but left an old jacket and some cheap lace to show she would come back. Prisoner never returned, and a warrant being iesued, Detective Gregory cleverly traced her to Tyldeslev and appre- hended her.—Mr Knowles, solicitor, Widnes, who defended, said that at a recent garden-party in Runcorn a lady told fortunes by palmistry, and the poor ought not tl be placed in the dock for doing what was allowed to be done under the umbrella of , the rich.—Prisoner was fined 20s and costs, or twenty-one days.—The money Was paid.

NUISANCE AT BOCK SAVAGE.

Mx J Thomas, Rook Savage, wrote under date July 8, 1901, lodging a serious complaint with regard to a bad stench which arose in the cenre of Mr Griffinas' field at the bottom of the bill there. The nuisance had been in existence some months, but he had not complained earlier because he expected it would have been remedied•

On the letter being read Mr Stoke corroborated the fact that the nuisance was a very great one. Mr Hough said that a copy of the letter had been for- warded to the inspector, and that he had replied it was a matter for the surveyors as it was connected with a public sewer. Subsequently on the motion of Mr Stokes it was de- cided to refer the matter to the surveyors, inspector,and a committee of Messrs Baxter, Darlington, Wright, Chadwick,Guest,and Stoke3,to visit the spot,with a view to effecting a remedy. Mr Linaker also promised to confer with the com- mittee.

THELWALL WATER SUPPLY.

. Messrs John White, Maclver and Company, Warring- ton, wrote under date July 9th with raterence to the Thelwall water supply, and enclosing analysis of water from Thomas Whitlow's pump. This wail, they stated, was used by several people about, including the tenants of the Ship Canal cottages near to. They were strongly of opinion that the proper course to pursue would be for tne District Council to bring the water to the I village from the house at Latchford Locks, and would guarantee, as far as the properties for which they were agents were concerned, to pay any reasonable sum required to make up the interest on the outlay. They thought also that probably Mr Stanton of Greenfield would j oin in this arrangement. Of course, they could not answer for him as they were not his agents. Mr Norman Taylor, chairman of the Thelw all Parish Council forwarded a copy of the following resolution passed by that body on the 15'h July :—" That the Rural District Council be requested to arrange with the Corporation of Warrington for the laying of water mains in the township of Thelwall and for the supply of water to the inhabitants of Thelwall from the new reservoir on High Warren." Admiral Parry proposed that the Warrington Corpora- tion be approached as requested by the Thelwall Parish Council.

J: Mclntyre seconded, and the resolution was unanimously carried.

DANGEROUS STEPS AT LATCHFORD,

The Clerk read the following letter :—" Examiner Office, Warrington, July 12th, 1901. Dear Sir,—l should be glad if you would draw the attention of your Council to the most dangerous condition of the wooden steps connecting Fairfield-road, Latchford, with the high level road bridge over the Ship Canal. The steps are in a chronic state of die-repair, and at the pre- sent moment there is one step entirely missing in the middle, so that a stranger coming down at night would almost certainly have a very bad fall. I myself live in Fairfield-road and use the steps daily, so that I can per- sonally testify to their dangerous condition. I have several times been given to understand that your Coun- cil intended to replace the steps with a properly built stone stairway or winding path, but month after month goes by and nothing is done. The matter is really urgent. If someone is so unfortunate as to break a limb here your Council may regret their culpable neglect when too late. Hoping you will rine this matter has the Council's early attention.—l remain faithfully yours, E. V. WALKER. editor.—G. F. Ashton, Esq." Mr Ashton said he had written in reply to the effect that the Surveyor had been instructed to put the steps in repair. .S4Mr Woodward : This matter was raised by Mr Pill- ing. Mr Pilling : It was ; and the Surveyor has informed me that the work will now go on all right. Tbis was corroborated by the Surveyor and considered satisfactory.

MOORE LASE OItiTETTCTION.

Mr W H 'Hunter, engineer to the Manchester 'Ship I Canal Co. ,wrote enclosing copy of correspondence which had passed between the estate agent of the London and North Western Railway Co., and himself re tbe footpath at Moors,—Engineer's Office, Manchester, July 11th, 1901. Dear Sir. Yours of the 4th inat addressed to Mr Wallis has been handed to me. I pre- sume that you yourself do not happen to be ac- quainted with the facts of the case between the Railway Company and the Canal Company so far as the Devia- tion Railways and their collateral works are concorned • The Canal Company has no responsibility whatever in connection with these works, and as the footpath as to which complaint halt been made by the Rural District Council of Runcorn forms a part of them, the Canal Company has no responsibility in respect of it. The whole of the works were handed over to the Railway Company by, the Canal Company in 1893, and subse- quently a capital sum which covered all extra charges for maintenance etc of the whole and which was fixed by arbitration, was paid by the Company. Further the Railway Company h lye 'actually repaired the steps, and have maintained the f. neing of the particular footpath in ques ion, and, as you are probably aware, in an action at law, which arose out of an ancident which occurred at a similar footpath at Moore-lane the Railway Company after having pleaded that the respon- sibility lay upon the Canal Company, withdrew their plea and settled with the claimant. If you refer to the book of reference published by the Railway Company in the year 1896 in connection with the proposed Moore and Warrington widening you will observe that the slopes and footpaths which form part of the work in question were scheduled as the property of the Railway Company. lam forwarding copy of this letter to Mr Ashton, the Clerk to the Rural District Council,—Yours faithfully (signed) W Henry Hunter, chief engineer.— Edward Mackie Esq., estate department London and North Western Railway, Euston Station, N.W. " Euston Station, London, NW. July 4th, 1901. Dear Sir, Footpath at Moore. Further to my note of March 12th, I forward copy of letter dated 21;t March, from the Clerk to the Runcorn Rural District Council also extract from the accompanying enclosure. The matter being one entirely between the Ship Canal Company and the Council, I shall be glad if you will deal with it and send the Council a reply.—Your truly, (signed), E Mackie.—J D Wallis, Esq., Ship Canal Com- pany, 20, Booth-street, Manchester." DEATH OF DR. McKENZIE, RUNCORN.

Dr McKenzie, of Runcorn, succumbed after a long and palatial illness, on Wednesday morning, at his residence, Belvedere, Runcorn. In his early days Dr McKenzie was a student at Aberdeen and Glasgow, and eventually took licence as a surgeon of the university of Glasgow. About 20 years ago he east came to Runcorn as an assistant of Dr Robinson, but a few years later left for the South of England were he practised for some few years: He returned to Runcorn -about ten years later; and opened a practice of his own. He always took a great interest in public affairs, and was at one time a member ot the Runcorn Urban District Council.

BOATING FATALITY NEAR RUNCORN.

A Case of Overloading.

About 930 on Saturday evening, a sad fatality occurred on the River Weaver, at Weston Point. A party of six young men were rowing along the canal from Rock S3.vage to Weston Point. When near Orme'S Crane the boat upset and one of them, Samuel Rawlins was drowned. The occupants of the boat were: Ernest Moorfield, 44, Irwell-lane. Runcorn; Alfred Rowlands, Rock Savage; William Talbott, Weston:Point ; William Henry Dutton, 15 Stone-street, Weston Point; Thomas William Hulse, Wcston Point. and Samuel Rawlins,aged 22,_0f Mersey-road, Widnes. . .

_ On M,oclay evening, Coroner R Dobson held an in-, quest, at the Weaver Hotel, Weston Point, whither the body had been removed- Mr Louis Rosenberg, repre- senting the River Weaver Trustees; Messrs J B Chad- wick and G Summerfield, the Salt Union, Limited - Mr T A Annett, the mother of the deceased, and the managers of Holy Trinity School, were also present. Ernest Moorfield, 44,1rwe11-lane,a member of Runcorn Boating Club, said he took a boat from the boat-house at Sutton, and was accompanied by Alfred Rowlands of Rocksavage, and by the deceased. They afterwards took in three young men from the bank of the canal, which was against the rule of the club. The boat was I only licensel to carry five persons. Mr Willismq, representing the club, said the secretary told him the boat was only licensed for four persons. . • .

Witness : Five is painted on the boat. Proceeding, he said at Weston Point they met the steamer Chanti— cleer towing the barge Mary, and these made a wave between two and three feet high, which dashed over the bows of the boat and sank it. Knowing deceased could not swim, witness seized him by the wrist and got him to the keel of the boat, which was now floating upside down. He shouted" Hold on, Sam," but deceased ap- peared to take no notice, let tgo, and fell back hate the water. Witness then seized deceased by the collar, and tried to swim aahora with him, but could not do eo,and he made for the boat again. The boat turned over, and witness and deceased went down. Witness, on rising, could only see one hand of the deceased, who sank, and did not again rise. A line was sent to witness, and he was dragged out..

By Inspector,Hicks : He:believedltbe steamer;whistled. No one, as far as he knew, hailed the steamer to slew down. The steamer was going at a good speed. He did not think the boat overturned through having too many in. It would have been possible for one in the boat to have hailed the steamer and him not heard it,as he was watching the bank. Inspector Hicks : Did the steamer and barge stop to , assist you at all ?—No, sir. Inspector Hicks ; Complaints have been made that these steamers put on steam when passing these skiffs. and when they are overturned and the people left struggling in the water. :simply laugh at them How far had the steamer passed when the accident &courted ? —About ten yards. And therefore the crew could have seen the accident ? —lf they had seen it they would have come to us. The steamer and barge belonged to the Salt Union and you are employed by the same firm?—Yes. Mr L Rosenberg, representing the Weaver Navigation Trustees, said the Runcorn Boating Club only paid a small acknowledgment for the use of the water, and un- dertook not to interfere with the trade in any way. Alfred Rowlands also gave evidence, and described the steamer as going from six to seven mdes an hour. It was usual for steamers to travel fast on a Saturday night in order to reach the locks before they were closed. . . . . . .

Ephraim Palk', captain of the Chanticleer. elected to give evidenc and was warned. He stated that when be saw the skiff deeply loaded with passengers, and on tha wrong sicl,3 of the canal, he eased off steam. He did not turn on steam agaia until he Saw the skiff clear of the barge Mary. He bad never seen a skiff so deeply laden be:ore. The Chanticleer was not going more than three miles an hnur. There were no bye-laws as to skiffs on the canal.

- The jury returned a verdict of "Accidentally drowned," and commended Moorfield and Rowlands for their conduct

Funeral of the Victim.

On Wednesday afternoon the victim of the boating fatality was laid to rest at Farnworth. The deceased was born in Widnes in March 1879, and was educated at West Bank School, under the tutelage of Mr Snockly, the present master. He was one of the cleverest lads in his standard. and his master was safe in pre 'dating that he had a distinguished career before him After gaining many local successes, all tending to the calling to which he purposed to dedicate him- self, he eventually secured a scholarship which entitled him to pursue his studies at Owens College, Manchester. While there his course was marked with success, and he gained the warm approbation of the principal and his tutors At the close of his College term he gained an appointment as assistant master at Holy Trinity Day Schools, Runcorn. While engaged there he won the esteem of all by his court- eous behaviour and aff ible manner, and none more regret his early demise th in his Runcorn friends, with whom he came in touch daily. At Widnes he held an important place in the social and religious life of the town. He was the founder of the" Borough Minstrel Troape," and was an athlete, too, of no mean quality, and no one was more enthusiastic In support of the national winter pa-tince. As a Sunday school teacher he was loved ty his class. At 230 Ithe procession moved to Hartle td-street Wesleyan Chapel, where the Rev H Barraclough aonduated a most impressive funeral service. The deceased's favourite hymns were sung. Including" Jesu. lover of my soul." The service ever,the cortege moved on to the'Farnworth Cemetery, There were present; Rev H Barraclourrh, Councillor T Smith, and Mr J B asnett, members of the Sunday school ; members of the Ru^cond Boating Club ; West Bank F C and friends The chief mourners were :—Mrs Rawlins (mother)* Miss Lizzie Rawlins and Miss Nellie Rawlins (sisters), Misse, Annie Newman, Lily Newman, aOl Esther Newman, Liver- pool (nieces), Mr Clarkson (Manchester), Mr B Moorfield (Runcorn). Misses Louisa Moorfield and Annie Moorfield. Representing the Holy Trinity Day School.Runoorn.were : Mr T N Annett (headmaster), with the assistants, Messrs Cox and Pickston, and the Misses Rummer, Annett, B Annett Groome, and between 50 and 60 pupils attending the school. There were also present : —Messrs G Varndell, S Gough, A Tauten, Hall- W Game', H Davenport, 3 Baynham, T Tifoot (representing West Bank F O) ; Messrs J Harper. E Johnson. A Turton and the Misses Roberts. Benbow, S Moore, Perry. Taylor (Hartland-street choir) ; Messrs J Basnett, J 8 Wither (the Sunday school) ; Messrs H G Johnson, T Coop and W Harvey (the Rnneond Boating Club); Mr Peacock (the Widnes School Board) ; the Rev R B Corlett. Messrs 3 Seddon, H S Snoddy. W Jones, and Bride. The bearers were :—Messrs J Hartland. 13 Hartland, B Hartland, J Stocker, J Harper, I Odgers, and J Basnett (superintendent of Hartland-street Sunday school). An exquisite wreath was also sent from the Borough Minstrel Troupe, two beautiful everlasting wreaths were sent by the teachers of the Hartlard-street Sunday school and the west Bank Football Club.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

STRANGE AFFAIR AT A RPLEY-ALLEGED ASSAULT AND

ROBBERY.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before Mr F. Monks and other magistrates, Thomas Garrity, labourer, 7 Spading-street, was charged with assaulting James Buckley Mellor, near the Arpley Cricket Ground, on Saturday night, and with stealing from him 2Ad in money.—Superintendent Monaghan said the complain- ant had been watching some fishing in the thre 0- cornered pit, and was returning home alone by the cricket ground. When at the bend of the road near the river, prisoner came up to him and said, "Give me some money." Complainant said he had none. PriAoner took hold of him by the coat, " topped" him twice with his head in the mouth, and knocked him down and knelt upon his chest. Complainant called out for assistance. Prisoner put his hand in complain- ant's pocket while he was on the ground,and afterwards made off across the fields. Complainant, who was in a most exhausted conditicn and covered with dust, went in search of a policeman. A lady and gentlemen came up to him, and brushed his clothes He afterwards met two men, and pointed the prisoner out to them. They watched him until a policeman arrived, and arrested him. When charged at the Police Station, be replied : "1 don't remember whether I did it or not." He (Superintendent Monaghan) asked for a remand until Wednesday, for the production of the two men whom complainant met after being assaulted.—This was granted. Garrity was again brought before the Court on Wednesday when the Chief Constable explained that Messrs Browne and Crompton had been instructed to defend prisoner and be (the Chief Constable) was informed that neither member of the firm could attend owing to prior engagements. They had asked for a further adjournment until Saturday. and he (Mr Talbot) did not object to that course being taken. The magistrates agreed and prisoner was accordingly remanded until to-day.

A DETERMINED ATTEMPT AT SUICIDE. Warrington Compositor Hacks Himself with a Razor.

David William Jones, a compositor, lodging at 14, Nichol- son-street, appeared before the Warrington magistrates on Tuesday, to answer to the charge of attempting to commit suicide on July sth.—Mr. T S Steel represented the defend- ant The Chief Constable stated that the defendant lodged at the house of Mrs Jones, 14, Nicholson-street, and appeared to have been a very respectable and well conducted young man. On July 4th he left work a little earlier than usual and complained to a fellow lidger:of pains in the head. He went to bed apparently in his usual health with the exception of the pains in his head and everything went well until the small hours of Friday morning when the fellow lodger awoke and missed Jows from the bedroom which they shared. For some time he did not think there was anything unusual in this, as the defendant was in the habit of getting up some- times and going for a walk before. he went to his employ- ment. About six o'clock however, he became anxious and went downstairs. Not finding Jones in the house he went outside and found him in the outhouse in a terrible plight. He was in a sitting position, surrounded by a pool of blood, and had two razors in his possession. There were two ghastly wounds in his throat, one being five inches long, and another wound had been inflicted under the nipple of the breast. There were other minor wounds and the man seemed to have been hacking at himself like a maniac. Dr Joseph was called and subsequently Jones was removed to the Infirmary where he had remained until that morning. He (Mr Talbot) understood from Mr Steel, that acting on medical advice, the defendant's relatives had declined the heavy respon- sibility of taking charge of him. Dr Joseph would tell the Bench that at the time the man committed the rash and deplorable act he was certainly not in his right mind nor was he for some considerable time after. He (ffr Talbot) suggested that the best course would be to withdraw the charge of attempting to commit suicide and charge the defendant with being a person of unsound mind not under proper control. An order could then be made for his detention in the Workhouse for a fortnight and his mental condition would be observed. Idr Steel stated that he had been instructed to appear by the minister and deacons of the Welsh Church where the defendant was a worshipper. He quite agreed with the suggestion of the Chief Constable. Dr Joseph was called and intormed the Court that when he saw ljl,the defendant' on July sth, after he had inflicted the wounds be was maniacal and had to be held down whilst the wounds were dressed. He had attended the man previously and he appeared to be melancholy then. The pains in the head might have something to do with his state of mind.—ln answer to Mr Steel the doctor said he was aware that Jones was in the same compartment as the boy who fell out of the train on the way from Southport to Warrington on Walking Day. The excitement might have affected his mind. Witness was of the opinion that it would be a very serious responsibility for the relatives to undertake to look after him. Mr Pugh Griffiths, solicitor of Carnarvoid, represented the relatives and agreed to the arrangement. Defendant was accordingly sent to the Workhouse Hospital for 14 days.

For days together I was afraid to eat anything, as directly after- wards I was sei zxl with most horrible pains and other distressing symptoms of indigestion and biliousness. The least effort brought on shortness of breath, and I left me for some time as weak as a baby. Palpitation also alarmed me considerably. gested load on my stomach. Jed down with a dull, tired fee,ing, a splitting headache, and chronic constipation. When I was in the depths of despair I saw in the paper the wonder- ful way in which Chas Forde's Bile Beans had cured a cise just like my own. COMMON SENSE IN EXERCISE.

-- Probably no sane person would deny the value of exercise. Practically every one believes that exercise is essential to health. But few people get one-quarter of the exercise that they could. For that reason it is important to consider under what conditions exercise is least tiring, ar.d. therefore, under what conditions a great deal of exercise can be got through without ex- haustion. Health is, of course, all-important, and the state of health generally depends on the diet. To attempt any general rules of diet is futile ; what suits one man may be quite unfit for another. Sleep. again, is very important. After a sleepless night people soon eet tired. Occasional sleepless nights may be to some extent inevitable, but they are less fatal to endurance if the air of the sleeping room be fresh.

DON'T FEAR THE COLD.

- - a butan encouraged this atgr audt ht al warm sadly Gymnasia are often kept at a stupidly high Exercise in the cold, dry air is far less exhausting Peoplenthe w a should u al di r themselves, not by artificial means, but by movement. The air should be cool and fresh. tt ignored.ehma np ee rx a e tr:i se . e THE crcttsfs EXPERIENCE. Of mute°• exercise done correctly is far less tiring than exercise done incorrectly. Notice how any one who is learning to cycle will expend three times the amount of energy that the experienced cyclist does. All the different movements have to be done correctly, and they have to be combined correctly. It is this co-ordination, this division of labour, this economical way of working the muscles and nerves, that makes expert exercise far less exhausting than in- expert. The more the correct movements become easy and automatic. the less nerve force is needed. And. besides, these automatic foundation move- ments, if the expression may be used, relieve the mind from drudger?. As soon as they become natural and free from mental effort, the mind can give itself over to enjoying tbe exercise,whatever its form may be. fie who can bicycle without any thought about his balance, etc., can enjoy the scenery. From the heart we may learn a great lesson. The work it does is almost incalculable, and yet we do not I feel fatigue in the heart. Its actioa is rhythmical and regular. Rhythmical exercise is only half BEI tiring as irregular exercise. Hence music and singing may be a great help, and may finally keep away fatigue. USE ME BIG MUSCLES.

Another secret of endurance is to use the large mus- cles of the body as much as pofsible It is just as tiring to bend and straighten the little finger a thousand times as to do some huge movement, for instance, with the hips and shoulders, which may be a hundred times better exercise. I golf stroke may bring into play ever so many huge muscles, and yet be not in the least fatiguing. This is particularly so because the muscles are large and cap- able work. This ,if we consider tbe weight car'rhieois s vI carried, i,eannddhuyrtihnwegatiho kn telaal7 wof muscular exertion, must be accounted heavy work, is not tiring. Another reason why walking is such good exercise is that each Move- ment is a preparation for the next. _

_ VIRTUES OF LAWN TENNIS, ; An instance of this continuity of effort is the tennis stroke. An ordinary stroke may be made with the body facing sideways ; the hand moves from behind and above, and makes a sweep with the racquet, and then ends up with the racquet in theair and the body facing nearly forward This is just the position which we wish to have between the strokes of the games. Thus the stroke itself leads to the waiting position. Another secret of good exercise is to stop before fatigue bar+ reached a serious stage. Different exercises I require different Rules, but there are msny forms of exercise which are better done in a number of short spells rathev than in one long, persistent effort. Worry and hard work use up much nee ve force, and so leave less nerve force for exercise. But, on the other hand, exercise will free the blood from some of its im- purities, and thus counteract the effect of worry. It seems almost as if pure blood were as valuable to exer- cise as strong blood is. In conclusion, then, if you would take much exercise with ease and enjoyment, here is a summing up of the main points. Be careful and temperate in diet ; go to bed early ; breathe correctly ; use light clothing and proper E boecdo the x-rcise correctly ;especially practise the individual parts of it by themselves beforehand un— til they become easy and half automatic; play games or use some other form of competition; move rhythmi- cally; use, if possible the large muscles of the body and let each movement be a preparation for the rest; stop before serious fatigue has been reached, and in some cases, do a little briskly at intervals; last but not least, don't worry during exercise ; throw your whole beast I and soul into the pursuit,—"Science Siftings. WARRINGTON WATER- WORKS. Visit of Water Works Engineers. __- Description of the Works by Mr. Deas, A. M.I.C.E. Warrington was visited on Thursday last week, by the members of the British Association of Water Works engineers. The object of the visit was to in- spect the Corporation water works at Winwick and High Warren, where important extensions are now being made, The members of the Association have been holding the annual conference at Birkenhead and the visit to Warrington and N3rton tower comprised the afternoon's programme on Thursday. They journeyed by rail from Liverpool to War- rington,and were met at the Central Station by Mr Deas, Mr Mitchell and Mr Grey (of the Corporation Water works department) and several members of the Corporation. The journey to Win wick was made id waggonettes. Alderman Francomb (chair- man of the Water Committee) Alderman Monks, Councillor AJ Jolley and Councillor Baird accom- panied the visitors who evinced mach interest in I the construction of a new reservoir to have a capacity of 4 million gallons, antin the new engine house to supply an entirely; new ser- vice fur the higher parts of the district, de- livering into a new nigh-level circular service reservoir at High Warren, with a capacity of 14 million gallons. After partaking of light refresh- ments, provided by Mr James Deas, engineer to the Warrington Corporation, the party drove on to High Warren, where an inspection was made of the new reservoir in course of construction, and thence through beautiful Cheshire scenery to the Norton Tower, which, forms part of the Yyrnwy supply of the Liverpool Corporation. From Norton the party travelled by train via Chester back to Birkenhead. The catering at Workington was in the capable hence of Mr. Powys. The following description of the Warrington Water Works, from the pen of Mr Deas A.M.I.C.E , was supplied to the visitors:— In 1846 a Company obtained powers from Parliament .and the Appleton Reservoir in Cheshire was constructed in 1847; the engineer being J F Bat"iman ; the drainage area was 540 acres and the reservoir together with two residum- lodges had a capacity of 50 million gallons ; two filter beds with an aggregate sand area of 1,30 Q square yards and a clear scoter tank holding 350,000 gallons from which the main to the town starts. The level of top water in the storage reservoir wee 13361 feet above ordnance datum. The level of top water in the clear water tank is 114 95 feet above ordnance datum. In 1858 upon the advice of J F Bate- man, the level of Appleton Reservoir was raised three feet giving a capacity of 60 million gallons, and a water area of 24 acres ; a 24 inch culvert was also laid, con- veying the waters of the Hatton and Daresbury Brooks, with a watershed of 820 acres, the available flow being, however, limited to the discharging capacity of the 'above 24 inch culvert to the Appleton Reservoir. No extensions have been carried out at Appleton since the above date ; no compensation water is a iven from this `lreservoir,nor are we limited in any way as to the appro- priation or diversion of any springs or streams. In , 1868 further extensions were necessary so the Board of 1 directors called in Thomas Duncan, Water Engineer to the City of Liverpool, who advised the sinking of the welis at Winwick, in Lancashire ; these works' were carried out by C Ii Beloe of Liverprol as follows:— two wells were sunk to s depth of 150 feet or 40 feet below ordnance datum in the new red sandstone, one well within the engine house and the other immediately outside it ; at a depth of 50 feet from the surface a large chamber is excavated out of the sandstone, which chamber throws the two wells into one from this depth to the bottom ; from the bottom an unlined borehole 18 inches in diameter was carried down to 212 feet below it, or 252 feet below ordnance datum, but this was afterwards plugged up on account of the bad quality of the water from the borehole ; an engine and boiler house, service reservoir, cooling pond and 2 cottages were also erested. ln the engine house there is a single cylinder beam engine, the normal speed of which is 10 strokes per minute, the cylinder is 30 inches in diameter, and the stroke of the piston is 6 feet ; the engine drives two single acing pumps 18 inches in diameter and 4 feet stroke, one of them being situated in the well ; in the engine house and the other in the well outside ; small horizontal engine alongside the engine house acts I as a standbye for the outside pump. The engines were erected by Messrs Yates and Thom. of Blackburn, in 1871. The old boiler house contains 3 Cornish boilers, 6 feet in diameter, and 25 feet long, working at 40113 s pressure- The engine has a jet condenser and a small cooling pond is provided. TM service reservoir is built of brick and concrete and is rectangular in shape, it has a capacity of 2i million gallons. The tops of the covering arches of the reservoir have been formed so as to contain about 9 inches of water with the intention of keeping the water cool inside the reservoir. The level of top water in the service reservoir is 131 feet above ordnance datum. As soon as the Winwick works were available the use of water from Anoleton for domestic purposes was discontinued. In 1878 on account of the rapid growth of the town and district further works were necessary and Alfred Moore of Manchester was called in, and he advised a great number of works but the only one which has been carried out was an extension at Winwick. A new engine house was built containing two single cylinder beam engines by Messrs Daglish of St Helens, diameter of cylinder 21 inches. stroke 6 feet each engine driving a single acting pump, 17 inches diameter and 3 feet stroke, fixed in a well under the , house, at 15 strokes a minute ; a third well was sunk 80 feet from the other two, and connected by a tunnel, in the rock, a tunnel was also driven in a south-easterly 1 direction for a distance of 1,200 yards, the work being carried on from 4 shafts. The level of the invert of this tunnel is 38 feet below ordnance datum. The engines Are capable of lifting 2 million gallons per day of 24 hours. Previous to obtaining' our Waterworks Exten- sions Act in 1892 th'is was the last extension that was carried out. In 1890 the Corporation acquired the undertaking at the price of £278,000. The Appleton and Darestury Watersheds consist of well cultivated lands carrying a not inconsiderable population and in 1868 when the works at Winwick were constructed all domestic consumers were changed over from one system , to the other and Appleton water is used solely for trade and sanitary purposes, so ' that there is practically a duplicate system of mains through the town. Winwick water is also used for trade purposes but if possible and. if the water is suitable for the industry Appleton is laid on. The district of suoply is a fairly large one em- bracing an area of over 40,000 acres and the population actually supplied were in 1861, 34,105 ; in 1871, I 40,837; in 1881, 53,808 ; in 1891, 88,748; and in 1901, estimated at 80,000. From Winwick there is pumped' annually about 550 million gallons ef which amount 90 million is used for trade purposes and the remain- 'ag 460 icmest

leg _milliOn gallons for d,,.._...stia7l3drposes; from Appleton there is supplied 130 million gallons ; giving a domestic consumption of 460 Million gallons and a total trade consumption of 220 million gallons per annum. In 1898 the demands necessitated additional supplies both for domestic and trade purposes, and the Corporation instructed Mr James Deas to prepare the necessary plans, Sze., and to further assist the Corporation Mr Mansergla was called in, and piloted a Bill through Par- liament. Powers were obtained for the various works which are now being constructed from the designs and under the superintendence of Mr Dem, at an estimated total cost of £247,000. The new " domestic " works comprise an entirely new pumping station, at Delph- lane, with a pump well 12 feet 6 inches diameter, and 160 feet deep, at the bottom of which an adit of 6 feet high by 4 feet wide is being driven in a north-easterly direction for a distance of about 1,200 yards there being three working shafts in addition to the pump well. The contract for the well and heading complete was let to Mr George Lawson, Rutherglen, at £24,400. Tne build- ings will comprise engine house. 50 feet by 35 feet, i boiler house 50 feet by 19 feet : coal shed 50 feet by 19 feet ; workshop and store 50 feet by 15 feet, there will also be provided houses for the engine driver and stoker. The water from the new well will be lifted by means of a triple expansion virtical receiver engine actuating two deep well pumps, 18 inches diametet and 3 Feet 6 inches stroke, which lift the water to a cast iron tank under the engine room floor and from thence the water will be lifted by means of force pumps through If. miles of 20 inch cast iron rising main to the Vvinwick reser- voirs. The whole of the machinery will in the future be duplicated, the buildings ico., being made large enough for this. The high, intermediate and low pressure cylinders will have diameters of 35, 22i, and 13 inches respectively, and steam will (be supplied at a pressure of 1601bs. per square inch from two Lancashire boilers 18 feet hy 7 feet, [laving Sirnpsons' Superheaters and Greens' Economiser attached. The engines and pumps are to be capable when running at their normal speed of raising 3 million gallons of water per 24 hours , against 250 feet,and the duty guarantees are as follow : —13.9 lbs. et steam per pump horse power hour 1.5 !be of Welsh steam coal per pump horse power hour. The plant is being supplied by Messrs J Simpson and Co, London, the contract mice being £11,175. The rising main is being laid by Messrs Jowett Bros, Burscough, the contract price being £1,940, the pipes and specials being supplied by the Staveley Coal and Iron Company, Limited, at a contract price of £4,330. At Winwick the service reservoir accommodasion is being increased by a new reservoir whose size is 215 feet by 190 feet and depth 20 feet ; its capacity is ai million gallons and top water level 131,35 feet above ordnance datum. It is entirely of concrete, the roof being formed of concrete arches supported on 11 cross walls. The floor and main walls are lined with Callender's bitumen sheeting, with a facing of inch of blue, wire-cut ruaboa bricks, and he roof is laid with bitumen sheeting to prevent in filtra- ion of surface water. It is provided with the usual inlet, cutlet, scour and overflow pipes and connections to the existing reservoir. This reservoir along with boundary walls, roads, etc. is let to Mr Georgelßell, Manchester, at £22,000. A 27 inch vEnturi meter will

be fixed on the service pipe to the town, so that the rate nf consumption may be continu- ously recorded. An entirely new service Is being put down for the higher parts of the supply district and a pair of Worthington pumps are being fixed in an engine house close to the Winwick Service Reservoirs: The enable house, which is being built by Messrs it W Collin,,Warrington, at £14786, has a com- mittee room and other necessary offices attached; for providing steam for these new engines also the old ones a new boiler house and coal store has been built ;.the boiler house contains 2 Lancashire boilers 30 feet long by 8 feet diameter, and stesaLwill be supplied at 150 lbs. per square inch, which pressure will be redisced down to 40 lbs. for the old engines. Toe high level ser- vice pumping engines are of the Worthington triple expansion type, having high pressure steam cylinders, 8 inches in diameter, intermediate steam cylinders 12 inches in diameter, 2 low pressure steam cylinders 20 inches in diameter, double acting plungers, 7 inches in diameter all having a uniform stroke of 15 inches; each engine actuating pump capable, when running at a normal speed, of raising half a million gallons per day of 24 hours througn 6 miles of 10 inches, and 8 inches diameter rising main, the water being drawn direct from the service reservoirs into a suction stand pipe in the engine house; The engines are being constructed by Messrs J Simpson and Co., at a contract price of £2954 and the duty guarantees areas follows :-25 lbs of steam per pump horse power hour, 3 lbs of coal per pump horse ;power hour. These engines deliver into a new high level service rnservoir at High Warren which is circular in shape 126 feet diameter at top-water level depth 16 feet and capacity I.± million gallons. The top-water level is 340 wet above ordnance datum, the floor, walls sand roof are of con- crete, the roof being supported upon cast iron columns, carrying rolled steel joists and arched steel channels, and expanded metal sheeting; a dwarf wall runs across • the reservoir so

_ that one half is supplying the -district, while the other half is being cleansd out. Ca!lenders' bitumen sheming is used for the walls and floor and also for covering the roof. The contract for this reservoir has been let to Mr C W Davenport; Warrington,at £6,748. The rising main to this reservoir and the service pipes to the town therefrom have been supplied by the Stanton Iron Works Co., at a cost of £6,700, and the whole of the pipes have been laid by the Corporation workmen. Where this main passes cinder the Manchester Ship Canal sub- way, it is formld of lapwelded steel pipes 10 inches diametcr, tested to 500 lbs per square inch in situ. These pipes Were supplied by Messrs T Piggott and Co., Birmingham at £515. All cast iron pipes throughout the works have solid lead joints no yarn being used, and in the case of pipes of 13 inches and upwards in diameter the whole of the joint is poured. The valves and apparatus throughout the works are being supplied by Glenfield and Kennedy, Kilmarnock, at a cost of £7OO. -,4There will be a complete:system of level trans mitten, indicators, and recorders for the reservoirs and wells, the instruments being manufactured by George Kent, London, and the connections, etc., are being I executed by the Warrington Electric Light and Power ICo., Limited. The " trade "works consist of a storage reservoir of a capacity of 170 million gallons on the Daresbury.brook, utilising a watershed of 900 arres.

This will be formed by an earthen embankment 800

yards long, and 65 feet maxhnum height. There will be the usual tunnel outlet and a line of pipes conveying the water to the existing filters at Appleton. The estimate for this reservoir is £lOO,OOO, and the general surveys have been made, and negotiations for the necessary land are in progress. At Appleton two new filters and a service reservoir will be constructed and the existing filters will be reconstructed. Workmen's cottages will also be built. It is hoped to commence these works this Autumn.

IS ENGLAND BEING SIDE- TRACKEO.

Another Letter from Mr. e. E. Parker. The following is a copy of another letter which Councillor Bennett (Secretary of the Warrington Cham- ber of Commerce) has received from the Vice-President (Mr C E Parker):—"Quebec, Canada, June 28th, 1901. Dear Mr Bennett.—The marvellous industrial develop- ment of the United States of America concerns us in so far as that development results in supplanting the trade which naturally belongs to England. The advance in commerce of one land does not necessarily imply a re- trogression in another ; indeed it may denote the very opposite.. It is only by examining the question in detail that we can rightly judge of the effect of American progress on our own national prosperity. Will America help to pull us along the track of national progress or will she in the expressive term so often need here " side track " us, i.e., turn us into a siding while she goes bowling by. Her larger population to some extent naturally entitles her to the premier position she has attained as a trading country. It will be a long time before the United States, per capita, has the foreign trade which England has I secured. Our country per bead of population does in exports over twice the foreign trade of which the United States boasts, and more than that of the United States and Germany together. When, however, we take individual markets and particular classes of goods, we find the United States making serious inrowis into iour trade. Our own exports (especially in manufac- tured articles), are practically stagnant. Some are per- ceptibly shrinking, and it is in these very things that the United States is rapidly increasing. When there is / anything going wrong in our national work-shops, it is a convenient and common practice to decry our fiscal policy and ascribe our state of stagnation and America's progress to the effects of free trade and protection. I have taken pains to examine this idea in the light of the facts in both countries, and my view is that the different , policies have very lime to do with the question, and that, at any rate as far as the question of exports is concerned, the country adopting the free trade system has far the better chance, and, other things being equal will secure the larger share of foreign trade. The ques- Clod I know is a very large and complicated one, and far too intricate to deal with exhaustively in a letter of this character. As far, however, as it affects exports it is comparatively simple. I would seek to illustrate how American exporters are hampered hy tariffs, and how, In spite of this additional weight, she is beating us on lines in which we are advantaged by free trade. Let me instance the question of the manufacture of boots and Shoes. All hides imported into the States pay al5 per Cent. ad valorem duty. This duty was forced into the Wilson Bill by the Western States with the idea of pro- tecting the stock raising interests. This naturally 'raised the outcry from the tanning interest that this duty would cripple the export trade in leather. With be hope (a Vain one) of pleasing everybody a rebate of 1.5 per cent. based on the value of the hide was given on leather exported made from imported hides, But it is clear that to have made things fair all round the process should have been carried further and a rebate should 'also have been arranged on exported boots, manufac- tured from leather made from imported hides. This was a practice impossible, and so the boot manufacturer is labouring, Under the disadvantage of having to , pay about 8 per cent, more than an English maaufacturer for American tanned leather. The American tanner, tanning imported hides, can sell to England so mush cheaper because he is recouped by the rebate above named. This is an instance of the anomalies and injustices which are inherent to a system of protection. But in spite of this burden which the American boot manufacturers have to bear, they are invading our home market and securing all the increas- ing trade in our colonial and foreign markets, while we, in spite of being able to buy the cheapest leather from all the world, barely hold our own. Naw. what is the explanation of, and the lesson to be learnt from facts like these P They are not far to seek and may be summarized in part as follows .—The Americans have developed a wonderful mechanical genius far in excess of the English. This, with practical adaptation of ways to means, abundant capital, and an intelligent, scientific and educated labour, has enabled them to produce at a cost and of a quality and style which knows no equal in , the world. Now, the sooner we in England trankly acknowledge this, the sooner will we resolutely apply ourselves to remedying the faults and failings which at present handicap us in the industrial struggle. I have already shown that it is not because of, but in spite of a protective policy, that America advances. If this required further confirmation it would be found in the growing trade between the States and Canada. Canada gives to the mottoes country a preference on goods imported to Canada of 33 per cent, as compared with goods imported from the States, and yet in spite of this advantage the import of this advantage the import of manufactured goods from the States is growing faster than that from Great Britain. I would in this connection refer again to boots and shoes. The bulk of the better class boots and shoes worn in Canada are imported from the States. On asking Canadians why English made boots do not take the place of those from America, I am met with the response that English made boots have no style, and are a bad fit. What is true of the boot and shoe trade is true of a dozen others. In Eoglish manufacture there is little effort made by those interested in manufacture to conform to the taste, style, and may be prejudices of their customers. What I would seek to impress is, that these and like faults are within the power of the English master and workmen to remedy, Were the energies spent in aimless grumbling over our fiscal policy directed to the overhauling of everything that contributes towards the successful manufacture and sale of our own products it °rim Contribute consider- ably towards maintaining or r.ational industrial prosperity,"

should ihuf^
