THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, JULY 19, 1901.

INTERESTING MARRIAGE AT PADGATE.

Richardson—Bennett.

There was a pretty village weddirig at Padgate on Thursday la,t week, The contracting parties were Miss liettie Bennett, third daughter of Mr William Bennett, of :,,yndhurst, Padgate, and Mr John E Richardson, son of Mr Edwin Richardson, Of Pad- dington House, near Warrington, and carrying on business at Bridge•street. The weather was all that could be desired. The ceremony took place at the Wesleyan Church, and a crowded congregation assembled. Flags and bunting were prominently displayed in close proximity to the residence of the bride's father, and over the entrance to the drive the motto "God bless the happy pair" was erected. Across the road near the Church considerable bunting was displayed, and the gateway of the Church was prettily adorned with ferns and fla were. Inside the Church, the com- munion rails and table were decorated with white flowers and ferns. Prior to the arrival of the bride and guests Mr W H Mus. Rae., played Schubert's air, "Andantino"... a, selection of Handers. When the bride entered toe church, leaning on the:arm of her father, who gave her away, the choir sang the hymn "The voice that breathed o'er Eden." The officiating ministers were the Rev John Gasmen, Peeketh, and the Rev HG Roberts,M.A. The bride was attired in a lovely dress of ivory satin delaine, with transoarent yoke and sleeves of real Floniton lace and mousseline-de- soi. The bodice was trimmed with orange blossom and myttte. She also wore a wreath and veil and a gold and pearl pendant,the latter the gift of the bridegroom. She carried a beautiful shower bouquet composed of arum Wiest, gardenias, lilies of the valley and white roses, interspersed with ferns of various kinds. She was attended by four bridesmaids—Miss Bennett, Miss C Bennett (sisters of the bride), Miss Richardson (sister of the bridegr ona), and Miss Spencer. They wore handsome creme china silk robes, with trans- parent insertions of Valenciennes lace and chiffon. They also wore picture hats composed of white crinoline and sweet peas, with black velvet bows, and carried pretty bouquets of sweet peas and ferns. The bride- groom's gifts to the bridesmaids were gold chains and pendants. Toe groomsmen were Mr Charles Richardson (cousin of the bridegroom), Mr T Stanley, Carter Mr William Hey wood, and Mr John Jackson (cousin of the bridegroom ) Atter the ceremony Wagner's bridal march " Lohen- grin " was played by Mr Payten, and as the happy pair left the church Mendeissehea "Wedding March" was given. The n'aly married people were showered with confetti and rice when leaving the church. The wedding breakfast, sepplied by the Warrington Cafes,Limited,was served in a large marquee which had been erected on the lawn at Lyndhurst, and 43 guests were preset.r. Later in the day Mr and Mrs Richardson left for Carlisle en route for a honeymoon tour in the Highlands of Scotland. The bride's travelling costume was cf beaver zibeline with bolero bodice trimmed with ' panne velvet and Russian lace, finished with gold buttons, and hat to match. - PLEASANT PARAGRAPIIS:

IT is surprising how many things come people de Dot learn from experience. Tun mills of the gods grind slowly, as a rule, but they sometimes get a inJve on that paralyzes some of us. IT is a singular fact that the man who knows it all usually disagrees w.t. the other man who knows it all. A MONOPOLY is a good deal like by a baby. A man is opposed to it on general princi iles until he has one of his own. OFTEN THE CASE.—" The minister weighs his words very carefully." "Still, he doesn't seem to realise how heavy his sermons are." AFTER THE ESTRANGEMENT. —Hobson : "Do you and Chatterton speak nowadays ?" Dobson : " Ho does—l listen." " WHY, I'd like to know," said a lady to a judge, "cannot a woman become a successful lawyer?" " Bdcause she's too fond of giving her opinion without pay," answered the judge. " OCT of work again, Pat: I thought that old Skinflint gave you a job?" "He did, sor ; but Oi'll be kilt afore Oi'll starve to death for the sake of kapin' aloive, son" " GEORGIE, Pin glad to see that you are polite and offer sister the oranges tirst."—" Yes'm— 'cause then she has to be polite, an' take th' little one." HE : " I have called on you seven years, Miss Waite. All this time I have passionately laved you. To-morrow or the next day I shall probably come up and ask you to marry me. What will you say ?"—Miss Waite (confused): "Oh, this Is so sudden!" IT WAS AT THE CLUB. —Waiter (at 11 p.m.): "There is a lady outside who says that her hus- band promised to be home early to-nlght."—All (rising): "Excuse me for a moment." MR. HOOLIGAN: "Shure, 'tis a molghty con- venient arrangement living in wan apartment, Mrs. Finucane. Woi, whin Oi want to go from the dhrawiog•room to the shtudio or the kitchen, Oi just stay where Oi am." ON reaching a certain spot the driver turned round on his seat and observed to the passengers: —" From this point the road is only accessible to mules and donkeys; I must therefore ask the gen- tleuien to get out and proceed on foot. THE MAGISTRATE: "Didn't I tell you the last time that you were here that I wanted to see your face in this court no more?" Weary Wat- kins : "You did, yer onner, and that is exactly what I told the cop." PERKINS: "When I knew Harry he used to paddle his own canoe; but now he seems to be a perfect wreck." Jerking: " Yes; but since then he vt married, and his wife is one of those crea- tures that like to rock the boat." HIS DEFINITION. Little Nell: "What is imagination, Willie?" Little Will : "It is what makes you think a bee's stinger is six feet long." liln RESCUE.—" He shall bite the dust!" hissed the wicked tyrant. "Is there no hope ? " faltered the heroine. "Hark !" exclaimed the hero. "Methinks I hear the clatter of hoofs. Perhaps it is a watering-cart." BANK DEFAULTER: "I would give ten years of my life to get out of this scrape." His Lawyer: "That's about what you will get if I don't succeed in getting you off." FARMER HORNBEAK "You know how old Zeb Flint hain't had no faith in banks, an' has been keepin' his money in a teapot in the barn where he could go an' gloat over it whenever he felt like it?" Mrs. Hornbeak : "Well, what has hap- pened, Ezry ? " Farmer Hornbeak (drily) " %Va- al, Zeb, hain't got no faith in barns now." MILLICENT: "How long did your trip to Rome occupy ?" Madeline: "Oh, a week altogether— there and back." Millicent: "And you saw everything?" Madeline: "Oh, yes; you see, there were three of us. Mother went to the picture-galleries, I examined the monuments, and father studied local colour in the cafés." LORD GLASGOW was once travelling by rail, and tendered a five-pound note to the booking-clerk for a ticket. "Put your name on it," said the youth, and Lord Glasgow indorsed it as requested, putting simply the name "Glasgow," as is cus- tomary, with nobles, and handed the note back. "Here, hi 1 you stupid man I" cried the clerk ; "I want to know who you are, and not where you're going to." HUSBAND (breathlessly)—"l am to start on a trip in two hours, and you can go with me if you can get ready." Wife—" Certainly I can. It won't take me more than 10 minutes to pack, and that will leave me one hour and 50 minutes to dress." "Now, children," said the Sunday school teacher of the juvenile class, "our lesson to-day tells us of the powers possessed by kings and queens. Can any of you name a still greater power?" " Yes'm ' • I can," replied one little fellow. " What, Willie ? " asked the teacher. "Aces," was the unexpected answer. DOLLIE—" I wonder why Love is represented always with wings ? " Mollie—" They are for him to use in flying out of the windew when Poverty comes in at the door. Don't you know that much ? Sentence of Imprisonment-

Considerable interest was manifested in the trial, in the Royal Gallery, in the Palace of Westminster, on Thursday, to which John Francis Stanley, Earl Russell, was duly summoned by the Clerk of Parlia- ment, his peers being his judges on the charge of having contracted a marriage with one Mollie Cook, in the State of Nevada, in the United States of Amekica, his wife being then alive. The accused pleaded guilty and was sentenced to three months imprisonment in Holloway Gaol as a criminal of the first class.

===== 0002601_19010719_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
With the Third (Warrington) Volun-

teer Contingent. The Work in the Transvaal.

The following is a copy of another letter which has been received from Private T Davies (son of Mr Coun- cillor Davies) who went out with the third, contingent of Warrington Volunteers :—Vryheid, South Lancashire Hill, June Bth, 1901 : Dear parents, we arrived here as you see on Sunday last all safe after two days' journey, the stopping place being at Blood River where we en- camped for the night. Our section guarded the left side of the convoy. We could see a party of Boers in Scbupers Nek, but these were soon scattered by our artillery. The Hill we are on now is just outside the town, and was taken a,few months ago by the South Lancashire Regiment. Well, after a weary journey, we have at last got into tents which to us is a luxury after sleeping in the open air. The first night at Vryheid was the coldest I have experienced since I came to South Africa, and we had to get up and exercise ourselves in order to get warm. It is a splendid sight here f rem the hill overlooking the town, the night posts being on the edge of the bill, it seems like look- ing from a battlement of a tower. We could plainly see Lucas Meyers' farm at a distance, and several others, and when locking through our glasses we could observe mounted Boers going is and out of the farm houses. This hide and seek business is be- ccming very troublesome and vexatious, and it seems very doubtful whether we shall ever have a good fight at close quarters. It certainly cannot be called war, and it is beginning to strike one forcibly as to why these men cannot be caught? Surely by this time our mounted men should he equally alive to the intricacies of the country so asrito be able to strike a blow with effect. But we men know but little of what is going on and! am longing for your letters and newspapers which I know you have sent me, so that they may throw some light on what is the opinion of the conduct of the war at home. We can only gather frogments of it here. Yesterday we witnessed what may be termed a great spectacle. About 300 Zulus and their chief came and visited us, yelling and performing war dances. Through his interpreter he told Major Adam tbat the Boer!! were taking their cattle, and asked if they might be permitted to fight against them, a request which was refused. If I may venture an opinion, these may be good fighting men in their own way, and amongst themselves, but , they would never do to be placed side by side with the steady. movable, fighting walls of Great Britain, and in this war they will no doubt be discarded. It may be a pleasure to you to know that I, and no doubt others have been recommended for promotion, but owing to the great responsibility of having to gue.rd dangerous positions from attack, they wisely I think gave prefer- ence to older Volunteers. We have much to learn, and our turn may come in time. Sorry to report that Richardson died of enteric ; we feel his loss very much, as he was the youngest amongst us and a smart young fellow. Whitfield and Woculhead (the latter being my pal) have I think been sent home ill. It is no use try- ing to save money out here, for it all goes in dainties of some kind, and in bread when short. Just fancy a loaf of bread weighing three pounds costing oee a shilling. Well now, I don't know how long it will be before this letter reaches you. I hope you have got the others I sent, and I wish the authorities at home would quicken 'the postal deliveries here. TOM.

TANNING COMPANY'S TRIP.—The annual trip of the employees of the Penketh Tanning Company took place on Saturday last. Blackpool had been selected, and a goodly number entrained at Fiddler's Ferry at 5 30 on Saturday morning for a day at the popular seaside re- sort. Blackpool reached,the party disposed of themselves in different ways until the return which landed them safely at Penketh at 10 p m. Glorious weather helped to make a day of extreme erjoyment for all. PRESENTATION. —On Friday night last a very interest. log ceremony took place at the Penketh Infants School. room, when a splendid marble timepiece, with brurze ornaments of Lord Roberts and General Baden Powell were presented to Sergeant A Baker, who it will be re- memtered, returned recently from South Africa. A good number of his friends were present and his com- rades of "F" Co., Ictendea in uniform. Mr J B Aitken presided.and in the course of a neat speech gave a short resume of the position of affairs in South Africa, and said that it was plain the war would not be finished until sterner methods were adopted. Bespoke in high terms of the action of the Volunteers in coming forward as they did. He called upon Miss Tinsley to make the presentation. Miss Tinsley did so in • few well chosen words, and Sergeant Baker made a suitable* response. Mr E G Medley also made a few remarks. At the con- clusion Mr L J Foster proposed a vote of thanks to Miss Tinsley for making the presentation and to Mr Aitken for presiding. Colour Sergeant H T Manley seconded this, and the meeting elosedaThe clock was supplied by Messrs E and A Eustance, and bore the following iescription. " Presented by Miss Tinsley on behalf of the residents of Penketh and Sankey, to Sergeant A Baker " F" Company, Ist Volunt-er Battalion, South Laneashire Regiment, on his return from serving as a Volunteer in the South African Campaign." Sergeant Baker left Southampton with the second draft on May 11th, 1900, and landed in England with the first and seeend contingents on June Bth 1901, promoted from Corporal to Sergeant while on active service.

UNPROVOKED ASSAULT AT BURTONWOOD.

I At the %Farrington County Petty Sessions, on Wednesday, before Captain Withington (in the chair), and Mr J Ball, James Haverty, Burtonwood, was summoned for assaulting John Kilshaw on the 6th July.—Defendant did not appear.alr E A White appeared on behalf of the complainant and said the offence with which defendant was charged was of a very serious nature. After the magistrates had heard the evidence he thought they would say that the assault complained of was a serious and iowardly one and that in the circum- stances there was nothing in the slightest degree to justify, excuse, or explain the assault. On Saturday night, July 6th, complainant was stand- ing with hit little niece at the door of the Elm Tree Inn, Burtonwood, about a quarter to eleven. His father was the licensee of the inn. Defendant i came rpund the corner, and without making a re- mark of any kind he rushed up to complainant and violently kicked him on the right leg. Immediately afterwards a police sergeant ad constable arrived on the scene and defendant went away. There was no juetification or explanation for tho assault.— Corroborative evidence was given by complainant and Lavinia Hamnett--A fine of 20s and costs, or in default, one month's imprisonment with hard labour w'ls infllcted.

Boiler Fitter Drowned in Sankey Canal.—Sad Case.

An inquest was held at the Warrington Police Station on Monday evening on the body of Joseph I Warburton (541, boiler fitter, 97, Liverpool-road, which was recovered from the Sankey and St. Helens Canal at Sankey the same afternoon —Bertha Warburton, daughter of deceased, said she last saw her father alive at half-past one in the afternoon. He went out fora walk, but would not let her go with him. He was brought. home dead at half-past two. He bad not worked since November last. On two occasions he had ben in an asylum, and had lately been very eccentric in his rcanner.—Samuel Garrett said that about half-past one he was bathing in the canal, and saw deceased come on tie canal bank and undress. He went into the water. He was going out of his depth and witness shouted " Come out Joe," Ile replied: " I will," and did so. Witness asked him if he could swim and he said he could. Witness went some distance away, and Was informed that deceased had again gone into the water. He went back and tried to find him, but failed. tie afterwards gave information to the police. He believed that deceased went to the water to bathe, and bad no intention of drowning himself.—Miss Warburton, recalled, said she had had occasion to watch her father lately. When he went out be said he would go for awalk, but would sot go far.—P C Wood, Sankey Bridges, said he re- ceived information at a quarter to two that a man was in the canal. He went there and recovered ths, body with grappling irons. Re knew deceased He had been very eccentnc in his ways l ately.— The Coroner informed the jury that there was no evidence the man took hi 8 life. The only evidence, was that be went into the water to bathe.—An open verdict was returned. Lustas BBST GL keg BOOTS Ayr, gl-to UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET.—Advt. VOLUNTEERS IN CAMP.

How they are Faring on Salisbury Plain.

Last Saturday and Sunday the members of the local Volunteer Battalion entered Brigade camp at Perham Downs, Salisbury Plain. They left Warnngton in two detachme rats, the first depart- ing at ten on Saturday morning ant the second at seven in the evening. The first contingent consisted of members of toe Mounted Infantry and Cyclist corps, and also a detachment of Infantry, 150 in all. A 'aro crowd assembled .at the Drill Hall and also at the Central Station, where the men entrained. The baggage was also conveyed in the train. The officers in command were Colonel Ridg- way, and Second-Lieutenants Haslain and Avery. Prior to the departure Colonel Ridgway bri,fly addressed the men. The train left at ten o'clock. The remainder of the battalion paraded at the Drill Hall at six in the evening, under the command of the Adjutant, Major Rooms. The men were in full marching order, and wore their field service caps, which were excnanged on arrival at camp for sun hate, which, should the hot weather continue, will be greatly appreciated. Large crowds of people ! assembled all along the route, and as the men marched to the station headed by the band, under the conduc.:orship cf Bandmaster Richardson, their smart and soldier-like appearance was the subject of general comment.. Ti,e Ist V.B. South Lancashire Regiment, under the command of Colonel J C Ridgway forms part of the 30th Brigade, under Brigadier-General the lion Sir Frederick W Btopford K C. 8., of General I Buller's staff. The other battalions fc.rming the Brigade are the let V B Liverpools, 2nd V B Ches- hire, and Ist V B Royal Welsh Fusiliers. The Brigade is 2,800 strong,the s'rength of the Warring- ton Battalion :in camp being as tollower—Seventeen officers, 39 sergeants, 27 corporals, 21 buglers, 656 others, total 770. The officers present are Colonel Ridgway, Major J D Buckton, Cap' (tins J A Edel- sten, F 1 Appleton, J D Fairclough. WITH THE WARRINGTON BATTALION.

[3y An Ofigcer.]

Punctually almost to the minute we left Warring- ton for our annual Encampment, which this year, takes place at Perham Dawn, Salisbury Plain. Alter many and sometimes long stoppages en route we eventually arrived, at Ludgershall at 5.30 a m Sunday, very sleepy and glad io leave the train to stretch our rather cramped and weary limbs. The camp is pitched in a beautiful bit of country. The individual who has never been to Salisbury Plain would expect to see a flat un- interesting landscape. Quite the contrary greeted our eyes. After a tramp from the station of about three-quarters of a mile we suddenly saw our tents Pitched on a gentle declivity of ths vast undu!a- ting expanse of partially wooded Down about a mile alsead.

The men were told off to their tents not as in former yews, 8 or 9 to each tent, but 20 per teLt• This seems rather too much but the tents are three times the size of the old bell-tents and there is really ample room and to spare, and the inn are very comfortable. Our total strength is 770 and we are the second strongest in the Brigade, The tat V B Royal Welsh Fusiliers having first place with 820. Or Briaade. the 30th, consists of Ist V B South Lancashire Regiment, Ist V B Rayal Welsh Fusiliers, 2nd (Earl ot Chester's) V B Cheshirs Regi- ment and the Ist V B Rings Liverpool Regiment. The men were all served out with Baden Powell's Sun lia'as and new boors to-day in order to be in good serviceable working order for the hard work thst is before them.

MONDAY.—The orders for Monday issued by Colonel Ridgway were :—Company field training under COM- pany Officers ; attack and extended order at 6 a in ; Commanding i_;fficers parade for field training at 9 a in; and Company field training, attack, signals, and section leading at 2.30 p in under the Crmparay officers. The at- tack adopted by our Commanding Officer is the one that was most successful with Goneral Sir Reaves% Buller in South Africa. The men show an intelligent interest in their work and, although it is unusually hard—ths heat being most severe—'Mere is no grumbling; We were on ! parade in the bl,zmg sun altogether to day, 6 hours, and this is the minimum length of our days' work fer the whole fortnight. The heat was so intense during the fore noun parade that the Colonel issued instructions that the officers and men need not wear their tunics and the whole battalion paraded in their shirts and trousers with their Moir, sleeves rolled up. This was a great im- provolneut and the extra comfort was much appreciated by all.

, TUESDAY—The Battalion to-day hat; been doing the same work am yesterday tx:ept that certain Companies were detailed for the defence of different positions and other Companies were detailed to attack tnem. Blank cartridges were issued to the men, and the work proved mot interesting as well as inmtructive.

Toe Battalion Orders for the day contained amongst others the following :—" The Commanding Officer has pleaaure in recording that Lieutenants HG Roberts and TMBr a Meat havepasseri examinations in military engineering and tactics respectively, held in May last. The mounted infantry and cyclists are being instruc- ted in scou..in,g and reconnaisance work, and they are eavering a large area of ground during these operations. ThP battalion is now settling down to steady hard work in all ndthtehelnyn bvnaare to d are taking a keen and intelligent interest

Warrington Wire Weaver Hangs Himself. 1 Mr S Brighouse, county coroner, conducted an I inquest at the Police Station on Monday evening relative to the death of William Holbrook (34) wire weaver, 50, Leonard-street, who committed suicide by banging himself early on Sunday morn- ing.—Eliza Holbrook said the deceased was her husband. Ile bad complained, during the last three months, of severe pains in the head. On Saturday she insisted upon hiM going to Dr Langdale. She weat with him and the doctor gave him a bottle of medicine. They returned home, and retired to bed about half past eleven. Deceased could not sleep. She remained awake until three o'clock in the morning, and then fell asleep. On awakening at eight o'clock she missed her husband. She went downstairs and could not find him. She then went to an outbuild- ing, but could not open the door. She obtained the assistance of a neighbour, named Crank, who opened the door and found deceaed hanging inside. On Saturday deceased said to her: "I feel that bad, Liza, I think I would be better dead,"—John Crank, joiner, 54. Leonard-street, said he was called to the house.by Mrs Holbrook, and on opening the door found the deceased hanging by the neck from a cord attached to one of the small beams. He at once cut him down, but life was extinct. Dr Langdale was called in, and on examining the deceased said he had been dead some hours.—The jury returned a verdict to the effect that deceased committed suicide while of unsound mind. WARRINGTON BOARD OF .GUARDIANS. Improved Dietary for Inmates of the Workhouse.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning. Mr A J Jolley presided, and there were alsa present :—Mrs Clay, Rev J H Riddette, Rev T R Matthews, Dr Murray, Messrs S Davies. A Jolley, G Pilling, William Houghton, A. Brooks' Thomas Ritchie, John Backhouse, William Mather, James Robinson, R Forshaw, J Bilhuge, T H Ashcroit, , a L Bent, wi' h J C Sutton (clerk), S Sutton (assistant clerk). VIE COST OF REVISING ASSESSMENTS.

Mr PILLING moved the omfirmation of a recommen dation by the Assessment Committee of extra payment to assistant overseers for servic"s rendered in connec- tion with the revision of cottage assessments in their several pa.-fishes required by the committee for the pun poses of uniformity and for preparation of lists conse- quent thereon* Mr A JOLLEY secon led. Mr W Manisa inquired the reason for this extra pe yment. Toe CLERK explained that additional duties had fallen noon all the assistant overseers in connection with tbe recent attempt of the Assessment Committee to have a uniform avessment of cottage property. The assistant overseers had to go round their parishes to ascertain the particulars of all cottage pro- perty and the revision caused extra work in the pre- paration of lists. The work also involved the attendance before the committee oh the assistant overseers on two and r von three occasions. The committee came to the conclusion that the paymews now proposed were reasonable payments for work done. The CHAIRMAN (to Mr Mather) : Are you satisfied P Mr MATHER : Not quite. 1 think it is far too much for the work done.

_ The CHAIRMAN : The committee thought that on the whole the charges were reasonable and equitable. Ele rni,ht perhaps add that the revision was the comple- tion—so far as it was possible to speak of completion in this matter—of an attempt on the part of tbe com- mittee to have an tquitable re-adjus•ment and re- arrangement of assessments and to put them on a fair footing as between party and party. Mr Marna: What is the total amount of the pro- posed payments ? The CLERK : £139 123 6. The work in Warrington was very hPavy. The recommendation Was then approved.

AN IMPROVED DIETARY—TEA AND SUGAR FOR AGED PEOPLE.

The Howe Committee recommended that the existing scheme of classification be extended by the formation of a class Bto consist of women of not less than 60 years of a..e whose industry and general conduct in the Work- house shall be fawurably reported upon by the Matron and deemed by the House Com• mittee worthy of such recognition. Inmates in clasil B shall be allowed 2cza. of tea and Bozs. of sugar weekly in addition to the ordinary dietary, this allowance to be suspended in the case of any inmate falling sick and being transferred to the hospital. It was resolved that litis al visable to make seine change in the existing methods of issue and preparation of the special weekly allowance of tea and sugar to certain female inmates and that as an experiment the Matron be directed to take charge of the dry tea and sugar allowed weekly to female inmates in class's A and B and to arrange for the preparation and serving of the extra allowance daily to the female inmates so classified at such hours as she may deem suitable. ------

'TEIETRAINING OF PROBATIONARY NURSES.

- -- The CLERK reported the receipt of a communication from the Local G3vernment Board stating that, they saw no objection to the appriintmect of probationary nurses at the Workhouse. The Clerk added that he wrote to the Bcard asking for leave Co make the appointment of probationary nurses without having to 1 report upon it to the Local G-Arernment Board. The ' Board bad replied that they were not prrpared to accede to the request so long as there was no resident medical officer. The Rev J El RIDDETTE aski•d whether ancthing was being done to impart special technical instruction to probationary nurses ? When the Bard was contem- plating the erection of a new hospital it was under- stood that something in that direction was to be done. The CHAIRNIAN : Something is being done. The committee have done the best they could under the cir- cumstances. They have sent out probationary nurses to attend the classes conducted by a qualifird medical gentleman held in connection with the local technical institute on physiology and one or two other branches of science connected with their work. He (the chair- man) though*. the instruction had been very beneficial. The Rev J El Riddette said he was very glad to hear what was going on. THE BEER BILL.

a. letter was read from Sir C Quitter, M.P., stating that the Beer Bill passed the second reading on the 27th March by the large majority of 112, and asking the Clerk once more tv bring the matter before the Guar- dians with a view to requesting the attendance and support of local members of Parliament to ensure the Bill becoming law this session. The CLERK said the local members of Parliament had written to him stating that they approved of the Bill 1: and he did not think it necessary to send a furtlyn. communication. ORDERS UPON TRADESMEN.

The special committee after the consideration of a letter from the Local Government Board recommend that in view of the success of the system hitherto adopted no change be made in the method of preparing and issuing orders upon tradesmen. They are of opinion that the change in the direction indicated in the let ter of the Local Governmant Board would lead to waste in provisions, increased confusion in the tradesmen's accounts and delay in the delivery of the orders. A letter was read from the Clerk to the Rochdale Union pointing cut the insufficiency of the accommoda- tion in the Lancashire County Asylums and stating that from their Union they had been obliged to send patients to two other different Con _Ay Asylums. The Clerk pointed out that the insufficiency of N uch accommodation I was universally felt but with the exception of one patient at Haydock all the cases from this Union were in Lancashire Asylums. r This was all the business.

PA DGATE.

FOREIGN MISSION PRIZES.—OR Sunday afternoon, Mr Henry Ward (superintendent of the Wesleyan Sunday school), presented the prizes which had been earned by the scholars who have acted as collectors. Mr Ward in the course of his address spoke of the good work that was being done by the Wesleyan missionaries in foreign lands.

Orford Church.

The annual sermons on behalf of the Day and Sunday Schools were preached last Sunday. In the morning the Rev G Vale-Owen (curate-in-charge), occupied the pulpit and la the evening the special preacher was the Rev W Bracecamp. curate of St. Peter's Church, War- rington,who urgel his hearers to rearm their responsi- bilities with regard to the education of their children. They as church people should support their voluntary schopls in order to keep out the Board school. In the voluntary schools they were sure that religious know. ledge would not be neglected, whereas in the Board school they had no guarantee that religious instruction would receive the consideration to which it was entitled. He underrnod they were £5O in debt. And he asked them to give as liberally as they could in order to liquidate the debt, and to go still a step further by beclming annual subscribers to the schools. He paid a fitting tribute to the noble band of Sunday school workers, wbe in the various pari,hes in the country endeavoured at very often great self-sacrifice. to inculcate into the minds of the children the blessed truths of the Gospel. At each service there was special singing by the choir, the base solo in the anthem, sung at the evening service, being creditably taken by Mr Walter Gorey. The offertories at each service were in aid of the famds of the day and Sunday school.

Mr Thomns Birchen pre.sided over the quarterly meeting of the Warrington Equitable Industrial Co-operative Society, Limited. which was held in the Co-operative Ht.ll, t.,aukey street, on Monday evening. There were also present—Messrs A H Rathbone (manw.;ing secretary), W Jeffrey, J Shaw Green (auditor), and a fair number of members.— The following rsport was submitted :—" The sales for the quarter amount to .220 979 Os Id., being an increase of £1,545 I9s lid over he corresponding quarter of last year. The profits, after providing for all liabilities, interest on capital, depreciation, Stc, will allow a dividend of th:ee shillings in the on members' purchases. The new branch in Lovely-lane was opened for business on Wedne•day, Jae° 26:1. Your committee have to express their satisfaction with the results of the two wetkW sales just complettA. They look forward with pleasure, because of the splendid position of the shops and the admirable adaptation of the departmsnts with specially large accommodation provided for customers, for a large development of the business lin the near future, and would ask members to kind- ly their influence in recommending their friends and neighbours to join the society, that they also may participate in its benefits.—The Chairman said they bad had another successful quarter, in fact a record quarter: The receipts had amounted to £20,979, which was nearly £4OO over the last quarter, and £1,500 over the similar quar- ter last year. They hat also opened a new branch store at Lovels-'ans. It had not been open quite three full weeks, but it was doing a business of £3OO a week. They had bought two shops at ritock- ton Heath which would need enlarging a litle. The shops were newly opposite St. Thomas's Church. As soon as they were able to get the tenants out of those shops they would commence business. lie said last quarter that they were doing something over £15,000 a year. This quar•er the dividend and in!erest would amount to £16,300. That meant that they were handing to 'he families of purchasers ..£l5 or £l6 a year each. The society was an incen- tive to thrift.—The balance sheet showed that the receipcs from sates had bean £26,979 Oa Id, whilst the expenditure for goods was .C 20,809 4s 5d.. It was proposed to pay a dividend of £3,778 lOs 9d.— The (Mailman moved, and Mr Jeffrey seconded, that the balance sheet be accepted, and it was carried unanimously.—Four members of 'the Committee, Messrs Jeffrey, Williams, Albinson, and Wadsworth' retired ; but were eligible for re-election.—On the proposition of Mr Emmerson,seconded by Mr Crow- ther, they were re-elected.

S. MICHAEL'S CHI7RCH, RUNCORN The annual sermons were preached on Sunday. The morning service was conducted by the Rev E S Oliver, who took for his text " Behold, my servants shall sing for joy of heart:. In the afternoon a flower service was conducted by the Rev W II Stables, who gave an address to the cldldren. Gifts of flowers were presented by the scholars, which were afterwards distributed to the sick poor of the parish. In the evening, when there was a good congregation, the pulpit was occupied by the Rev T S Lea, of St. Ambrose, Widnes. He took for his text, the words "Young men and maidens, old men and children, let them praise the name of the Lord." Before the service a neital was given by Mr W Groun,ls, cones jag of " Andante " and " Allegretto " (Mendelesohn) and " Cappriccio " (Caripocci). The choir rendered the Cantata Deus, by Vincent, and the anthem, " 1 will call upon the Lord," with the chorus, "Glorious is Thy name," by Mr zart. This was a splendid effort, the parts being admirably sustained by the following :—Messrs S Blundell, S Joynson, W Hop wood,and NI asters Percy Ridge, James Hill, and W Dutton. Special hymns we-e also sung. Mr W Grounds presided at the organ.

Warrington Field Club. A good mnster of members, under the leadership of Messrs Holbrook and Webster, visited Rixton Moss on Saturthy, and spent about six hours in rambling, collecting, observing the various forms of bird, inseat, and plant life, and enjoying to the ful the delights of an afternoon on the moss in ideal weather. An al-fresco tea, about five o'clock was very welcome, and stimulated some of the members to renew their efforts in capturing spiders and in- sects until sundown. The large and beautifully coloured caterpillar of the Emperor moth (Saturnia carpini) was found on the birch ; a number of very fine cornuta were taken out of the heads of the cotton grass, which affords facilities for the protec- tion of itself and its young,and has been freely used by this spider ; the rose bay willow herb was in full bloom, and its brilliant flowers were in great profusion. Annual Licensing Meeting. The Magistrates at the Borough Police Court on Monday signed a precept fixing the general annual licensing meeting for Thursday. August 22nd.
